tion, is far more likely to obtain the full measure of enjoyment than he who is careless in this re- spect. Every week I spend a couple of hours on a general look over my machine. and I always follow the same routine, which is as follows:—(a) Examine the belt to see that the belt screws are not pulling through. (b) Clean the sparking plugs. (c) Rub a wad of cotton wool round the inside of the car- burettor float chamber to remove any dirt there may be. (d) Oil wheels, forks, steering head, &c. (e) Verify clearance between valves and tappits. (f) Test all compression joints with spanner. (g) Tighten tyre valves and security bolts. (h) Drain crank case, flush with paraffin and re-lubricate. (i) Examine and clean magneto contact breaker. (j) Test cycle bearings for shake. Once a month I also examine the exhaust valve, remove the cylinder and clean the carbon deposit, and have a look at the tyres, filling up any cuts or cracks. Not only is this interesting work, but it makes a wonderful difference to the easy and satisfactory running of the machine.

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

ON RIMS AND TYRES.

Once plated rims become rusted, indicating that the plate has worn off, there is nothing else to do save to have them replated. The paraffin and emery process of removing rust from steel beneath the plating simply makes matters worse. Wood rims should have the mud.wiped off them when wet, instead of allowing it to dry. They should not .be greased, but varnished every two or three months. Never take a machine fitted with wooden rimmed wheels near a fire after a wet ride. The warmth may cause the wood to warp. Run a finger over the spokes occasionally and test them for tightness. If any are loose screw them up; a loose spoke may lead to trouble. Too narrow tyres are inadvisable, as they are conducive to ripping, and do not serve the purpose for which pneumatic tyres were invented—to give one the comfort of rid- ing on air. A good size for the light roadster is ltin., though I prefer lf in. A ltin. tyre requires a slightly smaller rim than a lin. tyre. not larger, as might be supposed. It is very bad for any tyre if it comes into contact with oil. If any burning or lubricating oil falls on the tyre. wipe it off at once. Don't have the back tyre as hard as a board, and the front so soft that the crossing of a small stone is noticeable. Pump both tyres hard. It makes running easier, reduces the chance of punctures and lengthens the life of the tyre. Vul- canising is better than patching, when a big cut has to be repaired. A MILEAGE CHART. Unless one has actually done it one can't realise how appreciably it adds to the enjoyment of cycling if a mileage chart be kept, the speedometer reckon- ing being recorded at the, end of each week. Espe- cially is this the case if one is able to compare notes with other cyclists. To make a suitable chart is quite a simple matter. Take a piece of double foolscap- paper, open it out, and divide it into twelve sections by means of lines running from top to bottom, and, head each of these divisions with the months. January. February. March, and so on. Divide each section again into as many weeks as the month contains. Next rule lines from side to side of the paper, thus making a number of squares. In the extreme left of each square write a figure, starting at 5 and going on with the multiples of 5 say, up to 150. The chart is then complete. The mileage covered each week is entered in its special square, not in figures, but simply by drawing a continuous line. Suppose that one does 100 miles the first week of January, the line starts in the hundred square. The following week one only does 60 miles, so the line runs down to the 60 square. The next week you do 140, so up the line goes to the 140 square. Thus each week's running is re- corded. Of course, if you are contemplating a tour in which you may easily put up three or four hun- dred miles a week you must make special allowances for it in your mileage column. At the end of the year one will possess a most interesting chart, in- creasing in interest when you have kept the chart for several successive seasons. TO ENSURE GOOD RUNNING.

The motor cyclist who looks after his machine in a careful manner, and gives it regular atten-

UNDER-INFLATION AND ITS RESULTS.

More harm is done to the tyres of motor cars owing to their being under-inflated than by any other single cause. To obtain the best results from tyres they should be tested every few hundred miles to see whether they are being run at a sufficiently high pressure. The deleterious effect of under-in- Ration can hardly be over-estimated, since it causes very rapid wear. It is not altogether easy to state in words what is the exact difference between a tyre that has been used properly and one that has been under-inflated, but I may say that the former has a live feeling when worked in the hands, and the latter' feels dead. • The feeling will be recognised* if a new tyre and an old one suffering from the effect of under-inflation are tested at the same time. In the latter case the layers of fabric become separ- ated, causing the dead feeling referred to above. CURING A FIERCE CLUTCH.

A little while ago a neighbour of mine who runs a small two-seater took me out for a spin, and im- mediately we started I noticed that his car was suffering very acutely from a fierce clutch. At first I thought it was his bid driving, but when I took a turn at the wheel things were just as bad, so naturally I wouldn't admit that the driving had anything to do with it. For a long time I failed to detect the cause. Eventually, however, 'I was able to trace it. The clutch engaged all at once, and there was grave danger of serious injury being done to the transmission. At last. I found there was a ridge of leather right round the cone, and this was butting up against the flywheel and occasioning the fierceness. It was not a difficult job to trim off the ridge. When tested afterwards the clutch worked as smoothly as could be desired.

IS IT POLITICS?

Guardians Pass A Resolution To The Government.

CHAIRMAN'S OBJECTION.

"This is a political matter and we have al- ways barred politics," commented the Chair- man (Mr. A. J. Jolley) in attempting to throw cold water on a resolution sent by the New- castle Board of Guardians to the Warrington Board, which held its fortnightly meeting on Wednesday.

The reso-lution was that "as the increasing cost of maintaining the poor who are dependent upon the rates results in additional taxation on the ratepayers, we, therefore, appeal to His Majesty's Government to inquire into and make every effort to facilitate the work of cheapening the necessities of life."

As soon as the resolution was read, Mr. George Mather remarked, "They don't tell us how it's going to be done. I move that it lies on the table."

The Rev. A. Id. Mitchell: I would move, on the other hand, that we adopt it. It would cer- tainly open up the whole question of the food supply. As far as we can judge we have been very negligent about the food supply. It is an opportune time for bringing it before the country. _

The Chairman: , But this is undoubtedly a political matter and we have always barred it. The Reverend Gentleman:, I, do not _think ii is a political matter. It is a national matter.

The Chairman: It is political in the ordinary sense in which we use that expression.—He added, however, that it was entirely for the Board to decide.

Mr. Siddelley thereupon seconded the rev. gentleman's proposition, remarking that party politics had been shelved for the time being, and they were all at one in recognising the interests of the country.

The Chairman: This resolution is contrary to our traditions. Party politics are almost dead, ,but this is the tort of thing that will revive them. There is no use blinking the fact that there is a wide divergence of opinion in the 'country as to what should be done.

Mr. Mather: The Government at the present time has more than it can possibly attend to in other mattera than these.

A vote was then taken, all members except- ing two voting in favour of the resolution.

When the Clerk (Mr. A. Bottomley) asked Mr. W. J. Shaw what side he was taking that gentleman was so enthusiastic for it that replied "Plural tote !"

COMFORTS FOR OUR SOLDIERS AND SAILORS. ,

A great deal has been done to make life a little easier for those who are fighting our battles at the Front, but it is important that theie be no relaxa- tion in this direction. It means little to those at home, but much indeed to those far away from their friends and relations, enduring the nerve strain of constant action or wearisome monotony. At no previous time were there so many on active service, and consequently never was the need so great. There is nothing so much appreciated as a gift of cigarettes, more particularly if they comprise those popular brands like Woodbines," Wills's Gold Flake, or Player's "Navy Cut" Cigarettes. The British-American Tobacco Co., Ltd., Westrainstei House, 7, Millbank, London, aW., makes up special parcels of these brands, and -supplies them at Duty-free rates. Orders can be sent them direct or handed to any tobacconist.' Fuller particulars can be had on application.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 17, 1916. WHITSUN eONFERENeES.

Tuesday at the Holborn Restaurant, London, Mr. Will Thorne, M.P., the general secretary completely byeaking down. He was about to reply to a criticism which had been levelled against him in a paper which he said should be nameless. "You have been told," he said, "that you have a secretary who is an , uneducated man. No one knows that better than myself, but I want to say that if the gentleman who wrote those paragraphs had been called upon to work at the same age as I was-"

He burst into tears, hid his face in his hand- kerchief, and sat down, the gathering giving him rousing cheers and singing "For he's a jolly good fellow."

SOLDIER-ODDFELLOWS.

Honour And Credit Of The

Order Involv-ed.

The Annual Movable Conference of the Man- chester Unity of Oddfellows was opened at Bristol on Monday. A Solemn Contract.

Mr. T. J. W. Siddall, Grand Master (Shef- field), reminded the delegates that last year they entered into a solemn contract with the members who enlisted that they should not be called on to pay contributions, and should not fall into arrears, and that those remaining at home should pay a levy of 2d. a month, so that no loss would be sustained by the Order on this account. The total contributions up to December, 1915, were £127,347 on behalf of 107,275 members; the levy will realise to December 31st, 1916, approximately £121,700, leaving a deficiency of about £5,600. Now that all their manhood from 18 to 41 is liable for service, perhaps half their membership would be called up, and as the contributions averaged nearly £1 a member they would "need to• review the• matter. The honour and credit of the Order was involved, and he felt sure that, while they might modify the contract, the pledge would not be treated as a mere "scrap of paper." _ _ .

Urgency was moved' for the consideration of a new rule relating to the contributions of mili- tary and naval members. It proposed that dur- ing the war members on active service shall be freed from payment of contributions, but shall not receive sickness benefit until the end of the war or discharge from the Navy or Army, and that 50 per cent. of their contributions shall be paid by an additional contribution of 1-d. per week made on all members remaining in civil life. , Military or naval members also pay their own contributions to receive full benefits.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
Altered Circumstances.

Mr. G. L. Lingstrom (North London dis- trict), who raised the subject on behalf of the board of directors, said it was thought that the matter was settled, but before many weeks it was found that it was not. Objections came in from all quarters, , some asking for exemption from the contribution Of a halfpenny weekly, others asking for a postponement of the levy. One serious objection was to paying for men who might be in a well-paid and "soft" job, and that was a difficult matter. It was, however, sad that objections had been raised, and he was glad to say to their credit, nine hundred and ninety- nine out of every thousand had paid. (Cheers.) Owing to changed circumstances since the war began there would be a heavy deficit. The new rule proposed by the board for registration was justified owing to the altered circumstances.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
Women Contributors.

Mr. A. Inman (Birmingham) formally seconded.

Mr. A. Culpan (Halifax) moved an amend- ment to limit the payment of the additional contribution to male members. He considered the Society had been unwise in asking women members to pay. Mr. W. D. Millard (Canterbury) agreed that many female members were not in a posi- tion to pay the extra levy, and he objected to them being told in a hard and fast way to pay up or go out. After considerable opposition the previous question was carried and the amendment was declared lost.

Mr. J. Ho%flings (Norwich) proposed an amendment that female members should pay a contribution of one farthing a week. The pro- position was received with laughter. The discussion was adjourned tin Tuesday, when, on a card vote, the new rule was adopted for immediate registration.

Past Grand Master's Son As Grand Master.

Mr. Wallace Smith (Liverpool) was unani- mously elected Grand Master for the ensuing year. This is the first occasion in the history of the Order when the son of a previous Grand Mater has attained to the highest office in Odd- fellowship. Brother Smith's father was Grand Master in 1869, and presided at the Sunderland and Seaham A.M.C. in that year. Mr. W. H. Hayes (Southampton) was elected Deputy Grand Master for thejear. The following were elected directors :—The Rev. F. Davies (Mitcham), G. Lingstrom (North London), J. Barley (Norwich), E. Bourne (Chel- tenham), A. Warner (Stepney), J. Hawkins (South London),‘ J. Smith (Edmondscote), B. Kilvington (York), and J. Coulthurst (Black- burn).

Amalgamations.

The conference decided on Wednesday that if the war is not over nekt year's conference should be held at Cheltenham.- Should the war be ended the meeting will take place in he Isle of Man. It was decided that a number of districts should amalgamate, among them being East Manchester, Eccles, Heaton Mersey, Manchester and Salford, Middleton, and Newton Heath. Permission was given to Heaton Mersey to amalgamate voluntarily with the Stockport district.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
GASWORKERS' UNION.

" Must Stand Firm With The Rest Of The Nation."

Mr. J. R. Clynes, M.P., presiding ov,er the Congress of the National Union of Gas Workers and General Labourers of Great Britain and Ireland, on Monday fn London, made a fitting reference to the war, its causes and the responsi- bilities as well as the rights of labour which might be borne in mind locally with benefit. Prussianism the Death of Democracy.

There is, he said, in his presidential address, every justification for pressing further upon employers and upon the country the claims of lower-paid workmen. No class has given to the Army and the Navy so large a proportion of men for the fighting ranks. Several rich men, it is true, set a fine example. They left wealth and ease, faced dangers and hardships abroad, and many are buried in a soldier's grave. But the poor were tfie_first to go in large numbers..

In' the sacrifices -demanded by the war the masses of the people are sustained by the con- viction that our country did nothing to cause this hateful encounter. Our Ministers tried by every means to prevent it. Our enemies gave no encouragement to either arbitration or confer- ence, and showed their preparedness for war by swift and perfect marching from victory to victory and from city to city in Belgium and Prance in the early days of the fight. Those who will speak only through the cannon's mouth have to be answeted in their own tongue. Our immediate and our supreme duty is to stand firm with the rest of the nation in resist- ing the dominion of German arms. Prussianism triumphant means the death of democracy and the enthronement of a military caste. The sacrifices of trade unionists made during the war in the national interests can never be repeated unless the advantages, trade customs, and rules. forfeited for the time being by organised labour, are restored honourably and completely at the close of the war. Who Forced Conscription?

An animated discussion took place on a report of the general secretary, Mr. Will Thorne, M.P., that he had worked for peace so long as he thought peace possible, and afterwazds did his best to obtain recruits by voluntary methods. He declared that the "destructive instincts of those who made voluntary recruiting a failure" were responsible for conscription being forced on the country.

This opinion was challenged by several dele- gates, but the paragraph was retained in the report by a large majority.

A New Title.

It was decided by a unanimous vote to change the title ,of the body to that of the National "Union of General Workers. In a statement issued to the Press at the close of the private sitting, the reason given for the decision was that the,union has changed in its composition from being a small body of gas- workers into a comprehensive organisation, in- cluding all classes of skilled and semi-skilhd workers. _

The union, which has a membership of 130,000, stated that 40,000 of the union's mem- bers are in the Army. Will Thorne in Tears.

There was a dramatic incident at the dinner of the National Union of General Labourers on

===== 0002601_19160617_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
ORDER OF DRUIDS.

Grand Master. Lavin And Insurance

Act Working.

In his presidential address at the conference of the Order of Druids Friendly Society at Blackpool, The Grand Master, Mr. John Lavin, of Han- ley, stated that the total membership of the Order at the end' of December was 157,102, and about 15,000 of their members were serving with the Forces. It behoved every branch of the Order to take steps to reconstruct their finances to meet the additional liabilities of those mem- bers, and also that no member might be penal- ised for his patriotism by having to accept reduced benefits when he returned. The relations between the doctors and • the approved societies were now, on the whole, much more genial than was the case when the new method of medical certification was intro- duced. At that time the societies demanded the maximum, while the doctors would only give the minimum, and not always that, but, by the use of a little tact in: converting a demand into a request societies had been able in most cases to Obtain the requisite information.- It was to be hoped that still, more cordiality would exist in .the future, although there were still some very obstreperous meMbers of the medical profession who delighted in opposition to approved societies. Juvenile Propaganda.

The importance of undertaking active propa- ganda work amongst juveniles to ensure the future success of the voluntary side of friendly society work was emphasised in the report of the Board of Management. The Board recorded having drawn attention in the reports of 1914 and last year to the advisability of branches of the Order undertaking a vigorous campaign to secure juvenile members and for the formation of juvenile sections in branches where they do not now exist. It was to be regretted, how- ever, that little or nothing appeared to have been done in this direction by the majority of branches, and the Board had again to report a lamentable decrease in juvenile membership. During last year approximately 7,364 maternity benefit= claims under the Insurance Act had been paid by the Order, and the Board ven- tured to say that if an effort had been made the great majority of children born might have been secured for some form or other of insur- ance.

Several delegates supported the Board's re- port. __

The New Grand Master.

Mr. Hardy Perkins (Leeds) was elevated from the vice grand chair to th 6 Grand Mastership, and the elections for otherr positions resulted as follows :—Vice-Grand Master, Mr. E. Stanley (Newcastle-on-Tyne); Board of Management, Messrs. J. A. Short (Sheffield) and William Braiid (Newcastle).

===== 0002601_19160617_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SHEPHERDS.

The Need For State-Appointed Doctors. -

The establishment of a State medical service was advocated by Mr.. A. M. Robinson in his presidential address at the conference of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds in Manches- ter on Tuesday. He said that there was abund- ant proof that many insure. persons were not getting the medical benefit promised by the Act. Carelessness on the part of the medical practitioners and the want of the essential medicines were the chief complaints. In the old days of their' friendly society work they were much better served. He was convinced that until the doctors were appointed by the State we should have little improvement. No fewer than 20,000 members, or 5 per cent. of the Shepherds' Order, obeyed the call to de- fend their country and their homes. Out of this number over 800, or about 4 per cent., had fallen in the cause. Juvenile Work.

Not so very long ago their juvenile movement was looked upon as an auxiliary to their ranks; but then they had greater scope for inducing those of riper years to join as adult members for sick and funeral benefits. Now that sick- ness benefits were provided under the Insurance Act their field of activity in this direction had become somewhat curtailed. They must, there- fore, endeavour to offer other inducements whereby the practice of thrift and economy might be brought home to those =among whom they worked as an Order. The immediate solu- tion of the problem, it seemed to him, lay in the vigorous and determined prosecution of juvenile work. Statistics of the Order.

The report of the General Secretary showed that one effect of the war was a reduction in the number of members. Some districts had been heavily pressed by claims for the loss of members. The lapses recorded this year were the lowest for a number of years, but, on the other hand, the admissions were less than they had been fora decade. At the beginning of the year they had 118 • districts. One had been expelled. This left them with 101 home dis- tricts and 16 foreign districts. Last year they started with 1,024 lodges. Three had been added, 19 had been amalgamated, and one ex- pelled, leaving the number at present 1,007. The number of adult members on 31st Decem- ber, 1915, was 135,157, a decrease of 3,099. The juvenile members numbered 62,916, an increase of 55. The year's income amounted to 1;235,487, and the payments to £202,677 leaving a balance of £32,810. The total funds of the Order reached the sum of £1,381,274.

• The report was approved. Members Serving With The Forces.

The Executive Committee submitted the scheme adopted at the last conference to the Registrar and learned from him that it could not be registered, on the ground that the general rules had not been complied with. The scheme accordingly was put forward once more with a recommendation that the rules should he altered to meet the case.

Mr. Saunders (Bristol) said it had been estab- lished that a stoppage of a soldier's pay in time of sickness was unless in the opinion of the commanding officer the man had contri- buted to his own illness.

A delegate front Cardiff thought the honest thing would be to"suspend all benefits and all 'payments of men serving with the colours. Mr. George Abbott (Sheffield) said they must have regard to the protection of the lodges. His view was that the lodges were better able to do without the contributions of members on active service than to carry on the liability for sick- ness and death. The Government had granted separation allowances, and in most cases the wife was as well able to pay the contribution as was the husband when he was at home. If the wife wanted to pay her husband's contri- bution she ought to be allowed to do so, and to claim benefit so far as sickness was con- cerned. As to the death claims, he thought they ought to be met without question of pay- ments.

The Committee's recommendation was ap. proved.

In his presidential address at the annual conference of the Sons of Temperance in Man- chester on Monday, Alderman W. J. FREE GARDENERS AND THE WAR'S DEMANDS.

At the annual conference of the National United Order of Free Gardeners' Friendly Society at Blackpool on Monday, Bro. T. Hill- yard, Leeds, referred to the fact that 6,890 mem- bers of the Order were serving in the Army and Navy. When the war was over, he proceeded, he was afraid their resources would be taxed to the utmost, for there would certainly be many claims made upon them by members returning from the Front broken in health. In the report it was stated that the total num- ber of .men and women insured members of the Order were 54,252 as compared with 53,131 a year ago.

'Mr. Abraham Smith, of the Bilston, Walsall, and Cannock district, was elected Deputy Grand Master for the ensuing year, thus becoming Grand Master-elect for the following year. Messrs. F. Hughes (Bedford), T. H. Hillyard (Leeds), and W. H. Hammersley (Wolveriihmp- ton) were elected directors, the office carrying with it a seat on the Board of Management of the Order.

A proposition to hold biennial instead of annual meetings was defeated.

It was resolved that the contributions of mem- bers serving in the,Army or Navy might be paid in either of the following ways: Either wholly by the member himself, or wholly by the Lodge, or partly by the member himself and partly by a levy on the remaining members. .It was decided to apply to the National Health Instr- ance Commissioners for an increased adminis- tration allowance owing to the great and unforo- seen increase in postages, which it was impos- sible to meet out of the present allowance.

WORKERS' UNION AND COMPULSION.

Lloyd George, Bamboozler.

The Workers' Union Conference at Birming- ham on Monday spent a considerable time de- bating a resolution protesting against the action of the President, Councillor J. Beard, and the General Secretary, Mr. Charles Duncan, M.P., in supporting the Munitions Act and the Mili- tary Service Act. Mr. Reader, of Bournemouth, submitted a motion .protesting against Councillor J. Beard and Mr. Charles Duncan, M.P., supporting the Munitions Act and the Military Service Act No. 2, and he accepted, on the, suggestion of the Huddersfield branch, the addition "without first ascertaining the opinion of the members." He maintained that the Labour leaders were not artful enough to see through the schemes of Mr. Lloyd George, who he described as the greatest bamboozler of the age. Mr. Locke (Willesden) seconded the motion. Mr. Watson (Salford) complained that with- out hesitation Messrs. Beard and Duncan jumped down on the side of the Government. Yet under the. Munitions Act the workers in the factories were suffering industrial slavery, and under the Military Service Act their com- rades would be suffering physical torment. Faced the Enemy.

Mr. Beard admitted that without hesitation he jumped down on the side of the Government, and said that if his opinions unfitted him to be president of the union let the members settle what they would do, because his views would not be modified; they would probably grow stronger.

Mr: Duncan alp° took full responsibility for his actions. All through, he said, he had in mind that there were tens of thousands of mem- bers of the Workers' Union in the Army and in the trenches, and he had tried to stand by those men who were facing the enemy. He was not going to see this country beaten in the war while there was anything he could do to strengthen the Army in carrying it to a success- ful issue.

The motion was carried by 33 votes to 12. Government Example Wanted.

At the conference on Tuesday a resolution was passed calling upon the Government, as the largest employer of labour, to fix local rates in- stead of leaving them to small private firms. Another resolution urged the Government to grant a minimum of six days' holiday per annum with pay to all grades of Government employes.

The conference instructed all organisers and branches to set on foot a campaign for organis- ing the dairy trade.

TRADE UNION RIGHTS AFTER THE WAR.

Solidified effort on the part of trade unions to meet after-war conditions was urged by Mr. Simeon Webb, of Dudley, presiding on Monday in Leeds at the annual conference of the Amalgamated Society of Sheet Metal Workers. He submitted that trade-after-the-war com- mittees .were formed not solely for the purpose of capturing enemy trade but to continue the present restrictive measures on the operations of organised 'labour with the specific intention of bringing down wages. He feared that the removal of " diluted labour" would be no light task, and would occasion many keen and bitter struggles.'

WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

The Secretary (Mr. B. Farrar) presided over the meeting on Sunday, and spoke upon "The Past, Present and the Future," making refer- ence to the late events—of disaster and gloom, our present outlook, and our future trust in a wise God, and His strong arm, when righteous- ness and justice would prevail. The Rev. T. T. James gave an address, after giving a welcome to two new members, also a welcome to Private A. Buckley, who was over on leave. First, he said, we all wanted peace, but there was much to do yet before that wish- ful time. The claims of our country must be met. England. was a great country. A love of country was great, and though we had to love all men, love for our own country came first. We loved it because it was our home, and the fact that it was ours. Love of country was on a right basis. Referring to the death of Lord Kitchener, the reverend gentleman said that what he had done for his country during the last twenty months could not be told. He had lived in glory and died a good soldier, and leader. We were not discouraged, but stronger for his courage.

JUVENILE LABOUR ON FARMS:

Part of Lord Selborne's speech at the great gathering of agriculturists at Worcester, dealt with . the question bf child

labour on farms,

Re said the right

line to draw was at 12 years; all boys over 12 ought to be on the land. Employment of chil- dren under 12 ought to be exceptional. Children over 12 should not be used except in cases where women were absolutely non-procurable. That view had been taken in most counties. Howqver, he agreed that our system of agricultural educa- tion required great changes, not in the direction of giving less education, but in the. directiort of: giving a •different kind of education. It was all very well to talk to him of. the old men who were such fine workmen, but they would have been much finer workmen if they had had a proper education.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN ON THE FARM.

The country is depending on its men for de- fence; it depends to a large extent on its women for supply, whether this supply be munitions or food. A soldier is no longer a soldier if he has neither shells or bread. Do our women realise this? Two hundred and fifty thousand men have been called out of agriculture, and more are going. So far some 50,000 village women (and they are mostly part-time workers only) have registered to fill this gap; and yet it is on these resident village women that we must depend for the bulk of the labour, because the agricultural industry requires scattered and not concentrated accommodation. Educated Workers.

Some hundreds of women belonging to the pro- fessional and leisured classes have come forward to avail themselves of the proffered short war trainings which will fit them to be full-time workers on farms. These women have proved that a strong, intelligent young woman without previous knowledge can, without injury to her- self, take the place to a large extent of the less skilled labourers on a farm. Farmers all over the country are now asking for the , educated women. There is a special mission• for such women at the present moment. Experience dur- ing the last few months has shown that women of this type, when carefully chosen and pla.ced, have proved themselves invaluable, not only because of the actual manual work they do, but because they have been the means of (1) bringing out the village women in many districts where for various reasons they were holding back; (2) causing farmers who had pre- viously refused to use the services of local women who had registered finally to avail them- selves of this source of labour. The Women's National Land Service Corps arranges for six weeks' trainings at a cost of 15s. per week, including maintenance, and has a limited number of free trainings to offer. Ap- plications are invited to the secretary at the headquarters of the corps, 50. Upper Barker- street, London, N.W.—L. Wilkins, in "The Times."

===== 0002601_19160617_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD CROP REPORTS.

The Crop Reporters of the Board of Agricul- ture, in reporting on agricultural conditions in England and Wales on the Ist June; state that the wheat crop improved during the month, and is now generally healthy and vigorous; still, how- ever, with the exception of some areas which had suffered frord the wet during the winter. Barley and oats have germinated satisfactorily, and both are generally looking well, although in the north oats ,appear to be the better of the two; the late sown also is mostly not so good as that put in early. The area under barley is slightly less than in 1915, but that under oats is about the same. Bean's are vCry, generally a strong and healthy Crop; peas are more variable, but are doing well on the whole in most districts. Potato planting has been very late, and is not yet completed in the north. Very little of the main crop is as yet showing above ground, but what is up appears to be satisfactory, and the early kinds are looking well. Very little damage from frost is reported. Reports of the hay crop, both seeds and meadow, are universally good. Pastures have now plenty of grass, and live stock have gener- ally done well during the month. The deficiency of labour is still greatly felt, and it is difficult to keep the fields clean. Women are being• employed to some extent in nearly every district.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

LOOSE SOIL AMONG GROWING CROPS. Few people seem to realise how important it is to keep the surface soil among growing crops loose and friable so as to admit air and sun- shine. If the surface is kept .well stirred but little watering is necessary. Should it be im- perative to water, then a thorough soaking should be given, following this up when the water has soaked away by running the hoe through the crop, and then applying a mulch of rotten manure, which will go far to retain the moisture and thus benefit the crop what- ever it may be. In town gardens the soil, owing to the drainage, gets very dry, causing the plants (I am speaking now of hardy plants) to flag badly if the sun is powerful. My rule is to give in the evening each plant a thorough soaking, afterwards watering the whole of the ground, using a can with a rose on it. The following day the soil is loosened up to a good depth, and it is wonderful how quick the re- covery is, and how much the plants relish such treatment.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
NEWLY-PLANTED VIOLAS.

It is strange how loth many people are to pull off all the blooms from violas when they are first planted. We invariably when plant- ing violas pick off every bloom, thus encourag7 ing the roots to take a hold; and at the same _ _ _

time helping the formation of young shoots from the base, which will in time flower freely. Not only the open flowers, but the buds also, should be removed. Do not -nip off the bloom only but remove every flower from the base in the axils of the leaves.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
BEAUTY OF ANNUALS.

Many people have but a faint idea of the beauty of annuals, simply because they do not take the trouble to thin the seedlings freely, and owing to this omission they class them as weedy and short-lived. A single plant of the cornflower, annual chrysanthemum, godetia, viscaria helichrysum, or mignonette will, if allowed room for development, form a plant nearly two feet through, will bear hundreds of flowers, and 'keep •up a long succession of bloom. This is the way to see annuals at their best, flowers from such well-grown plants not only being finer and of better colour, but last- ing much longer when cut than those from plants that have been crowded up and neglected.

POULTRY FOR PROFIT.

ADVICE IN BRIEF.

Tarring and creosoting should be done now. Make' any roofing repairs which may be required. Broken, glass should be replaced. Nettles and other coarse growths should be dug out. Where rate abound a day's ferreting is worth the time. New houses should be erected now. well

Keep the grass in the pens as short as possible; but use the hay carefully.

Buying Grain. Do not buy grain that has a musty smell. Wheat should be clean and heavy. Bruised wheat will not keep very long. The feeding value of thin or shrunk wheat is very small. Oats should be stout and heavy. Black winter oats are the beat obtainable. Avoid long, narrow oats. They are chiefly husk. Mixed grain is unsatisfactory. Usually the pro- portion of maize is too great. If a mixed grain i* desired do the mixing yourself.

How to Cult the Growing Stock. Throe months is the best age. They may theik be culled with certainty. Handle every bird. - Deformed birds must go. Deformities such sts crooked breast bones axe hereditary; so is side. sprig. , . If clean-legged .birds are desired., cull for feather ,leg. It, is extremely difficult to breed out. - Eye colour other than the standard is a great failing, particularly in the male birds. This Week's Work.

Hay stubble will soon be available; try to arrant, for the use of it for a few weeks. The birdsawould enjoy the free range. For the growing stock it would be of immense benefit. Adult stock intended for exhibition this winter should be induced to moult. Feed them sparingli and keep them confined to a warm shed. Linseed will, benefit the new feather. Paddy rice, boiled until the skin bursts, is also very good. It should be dried off with a little Sussex ground oats or fine middlinzs. Keep the birds out of sun or rain. —"Poultry World."

===== 0002601_19160617_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
NATURAL GROWTH THE BEST.

Notwithstanding the very unfavourable weather during the.ea,rly part of the rearing season chickens have made remarkably good progress. Progrees was ,slow at first, but , excellent development has occurred of late, and the season promises to be a satisfactory one. On most farms the bulk of the ohickens have now reached a stage when they are less trouble to look after; but this doesn't mean, as many would seem to imagine, that they mar be neglected in any way. If they are neglected they will show the effects by stinted growth and general ,ill-bealth.

, It does net really matter how greatly chickens for market are forced, so long as they remain healthy and develop rapidly. With the future laying and breeding stock, however, it is quite another matter. Their growth must not be unduly forced, and they should develop slowly, since only in this manner is it possible for them to grow strong and healthy. Breeding stock which has been forced is never satisfactory. , A little bone meal added to the morning mash is very beneficial indeed, providing as it does the necessary bone forming material. A tablespoonful among a dozen half-grown chickens once a day has an excellent effect.

As soon as chickens are old enough—and in fine weather, that is, when they are a month or so old— they should be given a free range. This is 2. matter of greatest importance.

Some Mash Necessary. The dry system of feeding is excellent up to the time the chickens are a month old, but when they have reached this age some mash is essential. Most of the chicken meals so largely advertised are practically similar in nature, and are excel- lent, containing, as they do, most of the elements required for assisting the growth and maintaining the health; so are coarse oatmeal made with boiled milk into a very stiff porridge, ground oats, and fine barley meal, from which all the larger par- ticles of husk have been sifted. Middlings are too coarse and fibrous for young chickens, and maY cause ,diarrhcea. There is one matter which must not be overlooked. Whatever kind of soft food is given it must never be top moist, nor yet must it be allowed to remain within easy reach of the chickens for any length of time. Make the food into as stiff a paste as possible, give very little, and at regular and frequent periods. This IS advice of a very elementary nature to the majoriti of poultry-keepers, and on that account, like most of the simple everyday matters, is frequently over- looked. Green feed must be provided liberally. So must animal food. There is nothing better in the latter connection than- ,worms. grubs, and in- sects, which, as a rule, chickens are able to procure for themselves..—Middlesex, in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19160617_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
AMONG THE CHICKENS.

June is a month in which several minor com- plaints are very apt to crop up in the chicken flock, the principal among which are summer colds, which may or may not develop into some- thing worse. It is easy to see how these colds commence; for one thing, June weather is terribb' variable. and uncertain, one day baking hot and the next wet and chilly; the long grass is nearlY always drenched with dew until about 10 a.m. (summer time), and it is the changes of tempera- ture that the chickens' bodies pass through on 'emerging from an often packed and stuffy house into the damp-laden, chilly morning air that is the chief cause of colds just now. Remove these causes and you have, of course, won half the battle, the evil lying in the fact that insufficient ventilation is almost invariably the trouble. Too many occupants are allowed to crowd into one house, and lids are allowed to remain fast closed by day instead of being left wide, open to admit sun and air.

Having taken this matter in hand at an earlY opportunity, those chicks with dirty nostrils that breathe heavily, or that have watery eyes, should be collected, housed by themselves, and treated as follows before they are let out every morning for a week. First wash away any mucus or dirt froni the head and clean out the nostrils and palate with a feather dipped in eucalyptus. Feed the green top or a fraction of the bulb of garlic, finely minced, in the soft food twice a day, and give a weak solution of glauber salts in the drinking water three times during the week. Unless the colds have already developed into roup this treatment should cure absolutely within ten days, and even roup will probably be vanquished before a fort- night has elapsed. If garlic be rubbed on sores and external warts it has a wonderful effect; if it cannot be procured onions are good, but not so good. - Garlic is easily grown, and should be in every poultry-keeper's garden, as it is a great healer and disinfectant. I understand that recent experiments in hospitals and at the Front have proved that one part of oil of garlic with two of water forms a better disinfectant than carbolic acid in that the garlic does not destroy tissue as the other does.—" Feathered World."

letters to tbt 03tritor

WRY VOLUNTEERS ARE NEEDED.

Sir,—Every Britisher, worthy to be so called, wants to end the war in the only satisfactory way—by v. tory. Is every Britisher taking his Part in bringing about this happy result in the Minimum space of time? The answer is de- tidedly—No ! Wanting it will not suffice. They Must all do their share, he it large or small— the utmost they are capable of doing. There are thousands of men who are hiding behind II badge, or who have obtained conditional exemp- tion from the Tribunals—some undertaking to loin the Volunteers when obtaining such exemp- tion, and who have not yet done so: many have ioined. May I appeal through you to those who are not exerting every effort in their country's cause, to come forward and put in their utmost at once, before it is too late? The expected victory for our glorious Fleet is

Partially fulfilled and only needs another oppor_

tunity to make it complete. This is apt tO rcon- ey a feeling of security to the inhabitants of this island, as the inference to be drawn is that we cannot be invaded, though there is no abso- lute certainty that an invasion may not yet be • attempted with some degree of success. Assum- ing that no invasion is attempted, then the general idea is that the Volunteers could be of no use and are not wanted. This is false. if the Government: were certain that they could rely on an adequate number of men sufficiently trained to guard the railways, waterways main roads, prisoners' and infernment camps, then they could allow more men to operate on the other side of the water, and any man who does not do his best to free the Government from its Present uncertainty in this respect, is clearly for selfish reasons, or through ignorance, pro- longing the war and aiding loss of life and limb; and treasure which is of less importance. The services of the Cheshire Volunteer Regi- tient have been accepted by His Majesty. The regiment, which is the first to receive this honour, is wanting more men in its ranks. Will those men to whom I have referred above turn a deaf ear to this appeal and remain indifferent to the number of their fellow countrymen who are killed or maimed? It is to be hoped not, but that all will realise their responsibility and devote a portion of their spare time to save Pi lriallY as possible of the brave fellows who are lighting our battles for us with the consequent risks of losing their lives, and leaving behind , them sorrowing widows and fatherless children. If not, when the war is over and they are asked What they did to accelerate victory, they will saY "I made munitions," or "I was badged," 'I had conditional, exemption," so could not loin the Forces. The. next questiop will be, ." What did you do in your spare time?" The answer, "Oh! I used to go to the cinema, or theatre, or I played cricket, football, bowls or 101 f, or did nothing." Surely it will be hard for a man (I say a man) to give that reply. Let kiln say "I joined the Volunteers so as to have tile privilege of defending my wife and family from the horrors of war as experienced in Bel- gium if need be, and to liberate as much of our -113-an power as posSible to bring the war to an 'early victorious conclusion, with the least 'arnount of suffering, and more I could not do." F. A. FROST, • Platoon Commander. Cheshire Volunteer Regiment. •Grap-, v nhall Hall, June 16, 1916.

RATE COLLECTION.

Increased Valuation Will Produce

Nearly £1,500 A Year More.

Councillor Wilkinson moved and Councillor Locker seconded, the adoption of the minutes of the Finance Committee at the meeting of the Town Council. The minutes were carried without comment. Bd.; composition and dis- count allowed, £2,606 4s. 7d.; allowances for empty property, £414 6s. 2d.; assessments re- duced, £m 15s. 114-d.; allowances for properties demolished, uninhabitable, and otherwise out of rating, £129 3s. 81d.; cases of ,poverty, which I submit for your consideration, with a view to excusal, £2O 66.; excusals in respect of the rates on the houses occupied by War Refugees, £5 7s. 5d.; loss in collection, £l6 Os. Bd.; arrears carried forward, £74 18s. 2d. The Poor Rate collected amounted to £35,746 16s. 10d.; composition allowed £2,244 9s. 3d.`; loss in collection, £6 12s. Id.; and arrears car- ried forward, £33 is. 7d. The total amount, therefore, collected for the half-year is £80,872 2s. 6d.; composition and discount allowed, £4,850 13s. 10d.; loss in col- lection, £22 12s. 9d.; and arrears carried for- ward, £lO7 19s. 9d. • Increased Revenue.

To enable you to realise the very considerable increase in the amount now received, as against ten years ago, I may say that the total collec- tion for the year just ended is, including the Poor Rate, £159,426 9s. 6d.; composition and dis- cohnt allowed, £1,369 6s. 10d.; the losses through bankruptcies and absconding persons, of:30 12s. 10d.; and the excusals, including the rates on the War Refugees' houses, £44 15s. 11d. In 1906 the amount collected was £106,253 3s. 10d. The present collection is, therefore, an increase of exactly 50 per cent.

The exCusal cases number seven. They are all persons in very poor circumstances, and, I am quite satisfied, are totally unable to pay, and upon whom groc-eedings for recovery would in- flict extreme distress. I think it will be con- sidered very satisfactory that we should be able to collect £160,000 and only be called upon to excuse so few persons of some portion of the rates they owe. The rates, although now stand- ing at Rich a high figure, have been collected with only about the average trouble, and except in some cases, fortunately few, the ratepayers appear to have been able to discharge their obligations without undue privation.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
Rates and Increase of Rents.

It seems apropos that I should here report that the new valuation list ordered by the Assessment Committee in September last was completed in time for the figures to be incor- porated in the rates now current. It was found that there was a net increase of rateable value over last year of £3,688, after allowing for de- molished property and for assessments reduced. On the present rates this increase will produce nearly .01,500 per annum, nearly equal to a lid. rate. Approximately, 14,000 rentals were ascer- tained, of which about 2,500 were found to have been unaltered during the twelve months; about 4,000 had been increased so much as to cause an increase in the rateable value, in spite of the operation of a lower scale of assessment, and about 7,500 had been altered only sufficient to counterbalance the scale. As there were about 10,000 alterations the year before, these figures show how considerable have been the move- ments in rentals during the last two years, and but for the operation of the new Rents Act there seems little doubt but that further increases would take place, for it is evident that the present figures have not yet reached the limit at which similar houses in many other towns are rented. It must, however, be pointed out that the large increase of the rates twelve months ago, from Bs. to 9s. 4d., was accountable for many of the alterations of the rents during the last twelve months.

Is Composition Scale Too Generous?

It may be necessary, before long, to consider whether the composition allowances are not on too generous a scale. 30 per cent. from the Poor Rate and 331 per cent. from the Improve- ment and Sewers Rate amount in the aggregate to .RB,BOO per annum. An Amalgamation of Rates.

I should like the committee also to consider soon whether an application should be made for the absorption of the Sewers Rate into the Im- provement Rate. The amount collected for the Sewers Rate is only £44 more than it would be as an Improvement Rate, and for this sum a separate rate has to be levied with all its at- tendant trouble and expense. It would be a very great convenience and save a considerable amount of labour if We had an amalgamated rate for all purposes, and the unification of the Improvement Rate and Sewers Rate would be a substantial step in this direc- tion.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 17, 1916.

WESLEYANS AND THE REV. H. T. DIXON.

Invitation To Anti-War Minister Cancelled.

MR. DIXON'S DEFENCE.

Although in March the Rev. H. T. Dixon was unanimously invited to remain a third year as pastor of Penketh Wesleyan Church, the War- rington Circuit Quarterly Meeting has now can- celled this on account of the reverend gentle- man's "attitude to recruiting and particularly the controversy in the Press he has engaged in these last few weeks." Mr. Dixon characterises their action as " abothinable, to say the least." Ever since the war began the reverend gentle- man's opposition to it and his sympathy with Conscientious Objectors have been well-known. He has expressed himself in private conversa- tion, through the columns of "The Examiner," his membership of the No-Conscription Fellow- ship, and in the days when Conscientious Objectors first declared themselves before the Recruiting Tribunals consorted with them in the Court and made a not inconspicuous and disinterested figure by taking notes of the pro- ceedings. "Although outside of the church I have made no secret of my opinions," he told an " Examiner "- interviewer, "I have never used the pulpit as a place for expressing them. All I have done there has been to preach the Gospel, which is the mission of the Church of Jesus Christ. The way I have been treated by the June meeting is absurd, for when the March meeting invited me to stay along with the other ministers they were fully aware of my views, which have not altered one iota. There has been no reason whatever why they should revoke their decision. They had the same facts to argue from as they have now."

===== 0002601_19160617_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
A LIVELY QUARTERLY MEETING.

The June Circuit Quarterly Meeting was held at Moore on Thursday of last week. "It was the liveliest quarterly meeting I ha•tre ever at- tended," explained Mr. Dixon. "Sixty-six voted against and twenty-eight for me. I pointed out that the letter I wrote defending the Conscientious Objector against misrepresenta- tions was one of which any fair-minded person might be proud; that to speak of a controversy in the Press was a misrepresentation, as, after writing one letter, I was asked• by high officials in the circuit not to pursue the controversy, and granted their request, giving as my reason to the public the Defence of the Realm Act; and that notwithstanding my granting their request, action was taken against me, which I considered was not 'playing the game.' In regard to my attitude to recruiting,' I asked the meeting, 'When every man from 18 to 41 is subject to conscript law, what does my attitude to recruit- ing signify?' But if they meant my attitude to conscription, then I was proud to have taken a stand against it. I characterised the whole thing as a little-minded attempt at Prussianism and an attack against the rights of the indi- vidual to hold his own opinions, for they admitted that I had not used the organisation of the Church for propagating my particular views about the war or politics." As Mr. Dixon said before, the meeting must have been lively. "There was no wrapping up of anything," he added.. "In this matter I have acted solely aocording to my conscience, and mean to abide by it. I am not ashamed of ray attitude; in fact, I am proud of the stand I have taken against Conscription. lam not going to allow anyone to dictate to me in which way my duty lies, and the only cause for regret I have is the sight of those of the Wesleyan denomination yielding a cardinal point in their doctrine—that of liberty of conscience. "Antagonism to me would be justifiable if my opinions interfered with the efficient dis- charge of my duties as a Wesleyan minister, hut I think no criticism can be levelled against me in that respect." RECRUITING SERGEANT'S INVITATION. Mr. Dixon does not mind a joke against him- self, because he invariably improves upon it. The interviewer remarked, "Welt, Mr. Dixon, there is this about it: if you do get out of work you can always find -a job in the Army." He laughed, and added, "Yes, I was once nearly in the Army. It happened last year. I was taking a holiday and had cycled to llttoxeter in Staffordshire, and discovered they were having a great recruiting campaign. I was standing in the market place dressed in a grey cycling suit and as unlikea ministeras anyone could be, when a Sergeant came up to me and said, Hello, man, don't you think it's about time you came along with us? You're a farmer, aren't you? You'll make a fine soldier. Do come, there's a good fellow! As he was talking thus per- suasively about a' dozen soldiers saw him and came to assist, taking me by the arms and help- ing me a few yards towards the recruiting station. It was with great difficulty that l escaped from their clutches and persuaded them to try someone else. They were keen on getting recruits." .

THE BRIDE AND BRIDEGROOM.

Arthur Isherwood, of Stockton Heath, and Private James Arthur Fairciough, A.0.C., pianist, and son of Mr. William Fairclough, took place at St. James' Church, Latchford, on Whit-Monday, the Vicar (the Rev. H. B. Firth) performing the ceremony. Both the contracting parties are well-known in musical and business circles. The bride, who was the Warrington Band of Hope T.Tnion May Queen eight years ago, was engaged at the National Insurance Offices; while the bride- groom, who for five or six years was organist at St. Hilda's (St. James') Mission, was up to the time of enlisting on the office staff of the Rich- mond Gas Stove Co. Naturally great interest was taken in the event, and a large number of relatives and friends attende,d. The bride, who was dressed daintily in white and carried a sheaf of lilies, looked exceedingly pretty. She was given away by her brother, Private Albert Isherwood, K.R. The brides- maids were Miss Elsie M. Whitfield (cousin of the' bride) and Miss Lily Isherwood (sister of the e s bid ). both of whom carried bouquets of o s sweet peas. Mr. Wilfred Fairclough randr (brother of the bridegroom) was best man. - A reception was afterwards held in Providence Schoolroom, Stockton Heath, whence the happy couple departed for Morecambe amidst the good wishes of the manylriends-assembled. Both the bride and bridegroom were the recipients of a large number of valuable and useful 'presents. I.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Business Women.

; Only quite recently have women joined the !Boards of Directors of important joint-stock companies, and trading concerns. The first woman in England to occupy such a position was the Baroness Burton, the head of the great brewing business, and she occupied a seat on the Board under the terms of her fathe'r's will. Mrs. Lazenby is another wonderful business woman, and a very hard-working director of the greatest pickle firm in London, whose success is in great part due to her untiring energy and business acumen. Lady Boot has done quite as much for her husband's enormous business, and has been for some time a director. But the Hon. Elaine Jenkins, daughter of the late Lord Glen- tawe, is not only a director of several companies, but the chairman of a railway company. Almost from her babyhood this young lady has evinced the greatest interest in business affairs of every kind, and whilst still in her teens, knew almost as much as her distinguished father, of the management, &c., of collieries and railways. She is quite as interested in the welfare of the workers, and help every useful movement in Wales, whether religious, social, or municipal.

To Help War Sufferers. I Miss Anna Airy, aided by an influential com- mittee of seven ladies, has arranged an exhibi- tion of women's paintings on rather novel lines, which ought to prove a great s.uccess. The exhibition is held in the Georgian Hall, kindly lent by Messrs. Waring and Gillow. The object of the ladies is to raise a fund to assist the many women artists who have lost their work, and their living through the war. All the money realised by the sale of the pictures sent in will be devoted to these sufferers, as well as to women who had taken up various arts and crafts as a career. The section of the exhibition de- voted to these is most interesting, and includes jewellery, enamel, copper and silver work, toys, and models of gardens, &c. Women painters, etchers, and black and white artists, have sent some of their best work, and in order to make the exhibition better known, social and, theatrical "At Homes " will be given regularly. Women artists have been hardly hit, and it is hoped that this effort may be crowned with the success it deserves.

An Aeroplane Elopement. In these days of freedom from parental con- trol, elopements have almost died a natural death. A young American lady has, however; adopted a novel Mode of " flight " from her home, and perhaps started a fashion in elope- ments, if not in weddings. Her parents refused their consent to her marrying a man in every way eligible, except that his profession—that of an airman—was: a dangerous one. • The couple decided to elope, and, ascending in an aeroplane, flew a distance of nearly eighty miles. Descending near a village, they were married, and telegtaphed the news to the parents. When the war is over the aeroplane will take its place in our social life, and it is possible that in this, country the brides of the next generation wip depart for their honey- moons as a matter of course in some kind of aircraft.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
•• :* • • 0:* •4 :* •• • 140 •41 0:* 4:* • • •:• • • Children' 8 Corner. • • Conducted by UNCLE ACE • 4. • 4. • 4. 4.

A WELL-SPENT WHIT-MONDAY.

My Dear Children,— Reading your letters explaining how you spent Whit-Monday has been a cheering ex- perience, for the letters are full of a desire to make the best of a sad time and to help others to do the same. I was particularly pleased to heat how many of my nieces helped mother be- fore going to the pleasures of the day. I was also glad to hear how the boys helped—not in housework, of course, but in the many ways in ' which boys can assist. When I was a boy—bat perhaps that would not interest you. - I should like to draw special attention to the letter of Nephew James Ryder, my very newest nephew, who went to Hill Cliffe on Monday, saw a ,nest of young thrushes and did not dis- turb them. James- is twelve years of age and goes to St. Barnabas' School. He is a credit to his years and his school. He is what I call a good judge and a real naturalist. The real naturalist loves to tee all life take its natural course; that is, in the case Of birds to see them thrive and sing in the freedom of the country, not languish miserably in a cage. One throstle singing his beautiful songs from the bough of a tree—where, as you know, he loves to perch and try how many distinct notes he can trill out in a given number of seconds—is worth 'more than all the caged or stuffed throstles in creation. And so James and his friend wisely—and kindly so far as the old birds were concerned—left the young thrushes to grow up to help to give the finishing touch to the beauty of our country- side.

The letters are so good that I have only been able to get out of the difficulties as judge by giving at least two prizes. These go to Nephew Arthur Williams (779), 1, Gladstone-street, who is eleven years of age, his birthday being last Sunday, so that his prize will be a kind of birthday present; and to Nephew James Ryder (818), 46, Elizabeth-street.

You will have noticed that owing to the needs of the Army all holidays have been suspended for two months, and that Walking Day has been abandoned for this year. Each school is to be allowed, if possible, to give a treat to its own children, as it is felt that the children should not lose their enjoyment altogether. This week I will ask you to write a letter on The Best Kind of School Treat to Take the Place of Walking Day.

I saw Uncle Ned a few days ago. He has been home on leave! He wishes me to say he is deeply interested in his old Corner and hopes to take possession of it again some day. We all hope he will—and soon—don't we? Because when he does so the war will be over, and that will be delightful.

Tht first little job I did was to harness the horse and put him into the eart, ready for my uncle and I to bring some coal. I then brought the cows out of the field because my uncle wanted to milk them. After dinner I took the cows back to the field, and my Cousin John came with me. By Nephew James Ryder, 46, Elizabeth-street. age 12; St. Barnabas' School : "Dear Uncle Jack,--on Whit-Monday the school children bad a holiday and many of,

Silk Jerseys. Anew garment has made its appearance which . .._ _

is intended to take the place of the sweater

that was so much worn in the winter. It is made of velvet and in shape is not unlike a loose smoking jacket. It has long sleeves and no collar, but this adjunct may be added if preferred. It is made in beautiful colours, and is in some instances trimmed with embroidery; in others with braid. On chilly June days crepe de chine lounge coats have been in great request for indoor wear, and have proved most comfortable. The new silk jerseys for summer wear are altogether delightful; they are- not intended for sports, but for wearing over a thin voile or linen frock, when additional warmth is required. They are made in the most charming shades of pink—one of the most popular colours this summer—and in every other hue known to fashion. Silk fringe makes a novel but most suitable trimming for this style of jersey; it edges the collar, the cuffs, and the ends of the sash. A thick silk cord is another mode of trimming; this goes down the fronts, round the hem, and finishes the pockets, which, as a rule, are very big. Silk-covered buttons the same colour as the jersey make a pretty finish.

This Season's Bathing Dresses. The short spell of hot weather that came in May brought out the new bathing gowns, which are as practical as they are pretty and varied. Many girls make their own, and perhaps noth- ing beats a blue alpaca, trimmed with plain or spotted blue foulard. A combin4tion suit with detachable skirt reaching in both instances just above the knee, is a favourite style. The long Russian blouse design is exceedingly pretty, and admits of many variations. It may be opened or closed at the neck, fasten in front, or at the side, have short or long sleeves, and reach below the knickers, or finish a few inches above them. In striped materials a yoke bodice can be . ar- ranged to give variety, and then no trimmings are required. The Russian blousck should in every case have a band, and the skirt is of course full; 'as even bathing gowns reflect the fashions of every-day garments. The mq,terials suitable for these pretty owns are taffetas, satin, alpaca, serge, tussore silk, mohair, silk or wool jersey, and silk poplin caps in a variety of shapes, match the trimmings of the suits, and pockets are a feature of these, as of ordinary skirts.

Smarter Hats. For afternoon wear the tulle or chiffon hat is decidedly a great success. It is small, semi- transparent, and usually finished on the top of the crown with a gigantic bow of the material, sometimes lined with a contrasting colour. Leaves and foliage are at present more in evi- dence as trimmings than flowers, but as the sum:her advances picturesque hats are to be wreathed with the loveliest blossoms, and flowers are exceedingly beautiful this year. The Dolly Varden hat has a well-deserved vogue, and a shape that turns up sharply behind admits of the use of a cluster of flowers, or gay loops of ribbons at the back of the head, where other- wise there would be an unpleasing gap. Open rush hats with long ends of ribbon floating behind are pretty and suitable ta the girl in her teens. All the new hats are intended to be worn well on the head, but not crushed down, as was the case a little time ago, the hair is therefore dressed higher on the top of the head, the ears being just covered with a slightly un- dulating wave.

them spent it very nicely. On Monday morn- ing my schoolmate and I had a wllk in the country for fresh air and then returned home for our dinner. After dinner we had a walk to Hill Cliff° and went to Bluebell Valley, where there were many bluebells. Near Blue- bell Valley there is a clear stream of water, and J slipped my boots and stockings off and had a bathe, and my companion lay dozing. The water felt a bit chilly at first, but came a bit better afterwards. We were studying science when three country Paddies came along with some ferrets and we watched them for about half an hour catching rabbits, and began to walk home afterwards. When we just got out of Bluebell Valley I came across a nest of thrush's young ones, but never disturhed them. We got home about six o'clock."

HIGHLY COMMENDED.

Niece Edith Pugh, 17, Ship-street, Brecon.— "We went a long walk to the Beacons. We first walked up a very long lane where wild fox gloves and harebells grow in profusion. We passed through a field 'where some sheep were grazing. As we ascended one of the beacons, we found heather was growing, the scene was entrancing. -The Beacons we found hard to climb as they are very steep; nearly exhausted we reached the top, as it had been our ambition to do so; and stretched around us was a wide panofama."

Niece Lily Williamson, 53, Priestley-street (Evelyn-street School).—" On the morning of Whit-Monday I awoke full of joy, for it seemed to be a nice day. I was quickly dressed and very soon I had had my breakfast. When the rest of the family had finished their breakfast I cleared the table and washed the dishes. Then I tidied up the kitchen. After that I began to think where to go. I decided to go to Victoria Park, but when baby and I were ready to go it became dull and gloomy, so I went to Bank Park. There I did my crochet, while baby played in her carriage. When I reached home my tea was ready, so I had it straight away. For a few hours after I played with my friends. When it was about half-past nine I began to get sleepy, so I retired to bed."

Nephew Harold Freaney, 107, Cartwright- street.—" I spent yesterday very nicely. I went for a walk. When I came to the waterworks I looked at the water: it looked like one big diamond. The trees looked beautiful, and the perfume from the flowers was lovely. I enjoyed my walk and it did me a lot of good." Niece Dorothy Wright, 68, Winwick-street.--

I am sorry to say I did not spend Whit-Monday as well as I thought I would. Monday is my mother's washing day, and she said if she got finished early she would take all the children to Stockton Heath for the half-day. Well, she got tip early and she had finished at dinner- time. When she had had her dinner she did not know what to do, because the weather did not look very promising. So she told me to run down to my auntie's and tell her that my mother was not going, and if the children liked they could go to the pictures instead. My auntie's children said-they did not want to go, so I took the children myself. In the middle of the third part it broke down and they could not get it on again. So the man said free passes would be given to agll the people to come and see the end of the picture the same night. When I came out of the pictures it was nice and fine, but windy." Niece Mabel Wright, 68, Winwick-street, had a similar experience, except that on Monday morning she went for a little walk to the Park

Niece Elsie Johnson, 323, Lovely-lane, spent the morning at home to see how the weather would turn out. After dinner she and her auntie and cousins went to town and for a walk, which was greatly enjoyed. WORKING MEN VISIT THE INFIRMARY.

Mayoral Recognition.

At the invitation of the Board of Management, the members of the Working Men's Infirmary Committee and their wives, numbering about 200 paid a visit of inspection to the Institution on Saturday afternoon. They were received by; the Chairman of the Board (Mr. J. H. Smethurst), who, showed them through the wards with the assistance of Messrs. Henry Collin, George Walker (chairman of the Working Men's Com- mittee), W. Daintith (vice-chairman), T. Wads- worth and R. Cook, the senior house surgeon (Dr. T. C. Cruickshank), the Matron (Miss C. Hammond) and the Secretary (Mr. J. H. J. Hampson). The company were highly pleased with all they saw of the arrangements made for the treatment an subsequent comfort of the patients. They were particularly interested in the operating theatre, the X-ray apparatus worked and explained by Dr. Cruickshank. At the close of the visit the company were entertained to afternoon tea at the Town Hall by the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), who is president of the Working Men's Committee. After tea, which was provided in first-class style by Mr. and Mrs. Mitchell, Raised £26,1300 for the Infirmary.

Mr. George Walker thanked the Mayor on be- half of the committee, and expressed the hope that what the committee had seen at the Infirm- ary would be an inspiration to them to proceed with the work this special committee had in hand. They must all have been impressed by the order and cleanliness which were every- where apparent in the institution. (Hear,. hear.) Warrington Infirmary compared very favourably in that respect with any other insti- tutions of the kind—in fact he had had the opportunity that week of visiting several hos- pitals in neighbouring towns, and he was sure the comparison was to the advantage of War- rington. (Applause.) The faces of the patients showed how greatly they appreciated everything that was done for their comfort. The coplitions generally reflected the utmost credit on the matron and the nurses, to whom the Board were deeply indebted for the keen personal interest they took in the institution'; quite apart from the fact that it formed part of their daily work. The Infirmary had at its disposal the best methods—medical and surgical—that it was possible to obtain, but it must always be remem- bered that its capacity for usefulness was limited by the extent of its funds. They had to cut their coat according to their cloth, and the success of the institution depended upon the amount subscribed.

The members of the Working Men's Infirmary Committee were grateful to know that the money they contributed was well spent; there was no question about that. He hoped that as the result of their visit they would determine not to spare themselves, in order that the insti.

Mayoral Presidents.

Mr. Walker, reverting to the question of the Mayor's hospitality, said the Working Men's Committee had no greater friend than Alderman Peacock. (Applause.) He had been the means of enabling the committee to raise about £lOO, and in the near future they would again have the 'advantage of his patronage and support in order that the funds of the institution might be maintained. It was a pleasure to them to know that he recognised and appreciated their efforts, and he was sure they all thanked him deeply for his acknowledgment of their efforts and the kindly way in which he had entertained them.

The thanks of the gathering having been un- mistakably expressed, The Mayor said he was the first president of their committee, but all the work had been 'done by their Chairman and executive, supported by the loyal colleagues they had on the committee generally. He had invitad the members and their lady friends to tea to show his appreciation of the services they were rendering to the Infirm- ary and through it to their fellow-men. 'With- out their help the institution would not enjoy the confidence it did, nor would its benefits be so greatly appreciated. The way the committee worked in a good cause was well known. ICnow- ing their worth he recently sought their assistance to get him out of a difficulty. The committee and the ladies' committee went so whole-heartedly int, the work in connection with the Hippodrome matinee that 200 or 300 persons could not be admitted. He was pleased to know that only a very few of those who were turned away availed themselves of the offer of the promoters to refund their money. He felt it a privilege and honour to be the president of the committee, and he hoped future mayors would recognise and appreciate their services. He was glad to know that the committee were so well satisfied with the working of the Infirm- He had been through it on several occasions, and had always been delighted with the way in which the patients were looked after, and with the way everything was conducted in the institution. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160617_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RAINBOW.

It need not surprise us that the beautiful bow in the sky has always something to do with rain, from which it takes its name. But it is interest- ing to note that much seems to depend upon the time of the day or the state of weather when it appears. According to an old rhyme a rainbow in the morning warns the shepherds to be prepared for rain shortly, and long; an evening rainbow points to fine weather go xt day. Sailors believe that a rainbow in the same quarter as the wind is a sure sign of con- tinued wet; if it comes in the opposite quarter the rain will soon stop. Again, if in fair weather a rainbow be seen, foul weather will speedily set in, but if the bow appear in foul weather; fair weather,will be at hand. Virgil would seem to have thought the bow uncom- monly dry, for he wrote (as Dryden translates him): "At either horn the rainbow drinks the flood."
