THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, JUNE 17. 1916.

A GREAT TERRITORIAL ANNIVERSARY.

Yesterday. was a notable, a mournful, and a glorious anniversary. for Warrington. It was on June the 'l6th, 1915, that our local I Battalion of Territorials charged the German 1 trenches at Hoogei performed the work they I were' sent to do, and paid the price that has to be paid when modern weapons are faced in , tht open field. The casualties in dead, wounded and missing mounted up nearly to the 300 mark, which at once bespoke the grim ' nature of the task the Battalion had been set and the determined manner in which they• undertook its performance. But still more grim evidence of the tremendims difficulties of the situation in which our local citizen soldiers suddenly found themselves is revealed in the subsequent history of the war in that particular locality. Hooge is part of. the Ypres salient, and for the possession of that salient the Germans and the British have con- tended, with varying fortunes, from that day to this. If anything, the Germans have made the most headway, and we fear the trenches the Territorials captured have long since re- verted to their original occupants. At the present moment it is thought that tile Ger- mans are preparing for an onslaught on the British here upon the grand scale. It will be remembered that the Warrington men were sent out to straighten the line in our favour. The Germans are now hammering at the salient so persistently that it is thought we ought to straighten the line ourselves by with- drawing from the outlying portions. "If further reverses are suffered," we are in- formed by a " Times " correspondent only this week, " the British line will have to be straightened. By doing so it would be strengthened, and in many people's, opinion it should have been done voluntarily over a year ago when the salient became uncomfort- ably narrow. A preliminary straightening might be on a line running through Zillebeke or thereabouts, though this would still leave ' Hill 60 on our flank. A bigger adjustment would lead us back to the neighbourhood of the Ypres-St. Eloi road. In either case Ypres would remain inviolate—only a sentimental asset, perhaps, but an important one, for Ypres has become to the British Army almost what the colours are to a regiment." So that the suggestion is that the salient which Warringtonians found too narrow and suc- ceeded in temporarily widening remains still - too narrow after twelve months to be retained with advantage. Therefore, we may fairly say that the heroes, whose death we rever- ently recall to mind to-day, or whose injuries we so keenly lament, did even more than could be expected of men when they endea- voured to do in a day what a year's efforts have failed to accomplish. Further than this, if no positive gain has been recorded at Ypres, there has been the very substantial negative gain represented by the fact that we have prevented the Hun from breaking through, and have assisted in wearing him down to- wards that breaking point from which he is not now very far removed. The Territorials were mobilised in August, 1914, they defended our shores for a spell, they went to France in February, 1915, they went to the Front almost immediately, and were in this greatest of all tests by June. They were thus hold- ing the fort in the early dangeroUs stages, while England was rearing her great Army, the Army that is now equal to anything the German can hope to devise. It was their readiness for immediate service that gave the Territorials their immeasurable value; it was the manner in which they did their duty when the time came that cast over the Battalion an undying glory. Let us to-day give a special thought for what they did for us a year yester- day. We shall feel better for doing so—and perhaps some of us who think we have struck upon particularly hard times will realise how • meagre have-been the sacrifices we have made.

Picturesqueness and sweetness were lent to the room in Bewsey Chambers in which the Rural District Tribunal was ,sitting, on Wed- nesday, by an abundance of flowers displayed on the main table. They were in harmony with the surroundings. The poppies matched the Chairman's red ink receptacle, the narcissi the paste-pot, and the blue pansies the faces of the applicants whose cases were dismissed!

Sid Nicholas, the Warrington half-back, has been spending a few days in Warrington this week, during which many of his' friends have been asking him what really happened at Suvla Bay, for he was there during 'the landing. Sid is a bonny fellow, and as good in attack on the battlefield, I should imagine, as he is in defence on the football field.

It is not everyone who will take off his hat to a public-house. Yesterday dinner-time I saw three Belgian soldiers remove theirs as they were about to enter an inn. But they refrained from bowing gracefully: they called a schoolboy standing near and asked him to hold their hats while they went inside. They remained within for a few minutes and then the boy handed the hats back in exchange for some coppers.

CAMEOS.

By The Schoolmaster.

Get There First. Complaint was made. I duly summoned the lad. "What did you hit Wilson for?" I asked. "Please sir, he was going to hit me, so I hit him back." "Did he hit you?" I asked. ".No, sir, but he would have done if I hadn't hit him," he returned. I excused the lad for his preparedness and resource. I wished I could always hit back first. There flashed through my mind the classic couplet "Thrice is he armed that lath his quarrA. just, But four times he who gets his blow in fast."

The Superman. Another flutter among the seven-year-olds. "Johnny Griffiths," I said, "this boy com- plains that you hit him." "Me too—me too—he hit me too," came from different parts of the class. "On the form, all those Griffiths has been hitting." Six duly exalted themselves. " Have you been hitting all these?" I asked. "Yes, sir," agreed Johnny. "Well, suppose they all wait for you after school, and give you a thrashing. What then?" Johnny looked carefully at each unit arrayed against him: every lad was about his own size. Then he looked triumphantly at me. "They daren't, sir," he said. What could I do? Nietzsche confounded me. I parleyed diplomatically: Johnny is now on my side.

Repartee. I believe in humour—good humour. I chaff and joke ray scholars: they reciprocate. A lad came to my desk. "May I have a drink, sir?'—" Yes," I responded, "look sharp." I continued my writing: looked 'up: the by was still there. "Look sharp," I said. "Wasn't I looking sharp, sir?" he asked. I thought hard for the fraction of a moment. "You must have been," I said, "that's one to Yrou, Reg." And he departed, chuckling. I got him later, though, with a smart one.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
Take Your Turn.

Ting, ting! ting, ting! The tinkle came from the middle of the class. I looked. The middle of the class was most intent on work—sure sign of mischief. I turned to write on the board. Ting, ting! I faced the class. The eyes of a boy in the centre sparkled: the corner of his mouth crinkled into the decimal of a smile. I went to his place, put my hand under the desk, made the ting, ting! myself on two projecting pen points. "Come out, Charlie," I said, "that's an old trick: I used to do that myself." Charlie held out his hand for the cane. "Please sir, you said you used to do it your- self," he replied. "Yes," I responded, "and when I got caught, I got the cane. Take your turn, Charlie." Charlie took his turn: the ting, ting! is now defunct: and we are all good friend&

Soldier of England, you who served her well And in that service, silent and apart. Achieved a name that never lost its spell Over your country's heart;—

Who saw your work accomplished ere at length Shadows of evening fell, and creeping Time Had bent your stature or resolved the strength That kept its manhood prime;—

Great was your life, and great the end you made, As through the plunging seas that whelmed your head

THE LOST CHIEF. Born June 24, 1850. Died on Service, June 5, 1916.

Your spirit passed, unconquered, unafraid To join the gallant dead. •

But not by death that spell COULD pass away That fixed our gaze upon the far-off goal, , Who, by your magic, stand in arms to-day , A nation one and whole,

Now doubly pledged to bring your vision true Of darkness vanquished and the dawn set fre, In that full triumph which your faith foreknew But might not live to see. O.S.

NOT CONVENTION.

Grief Sincere This Time, Says Ite‘.

J. M. Buckmaster.

At the services at. the Parish Church on Sunday the Russian Contakion of the Departed "Give Rest, 0 Christ, to Thy servants with Thy Saints" was sung in remembrance of Lord Kitchener and of the officers and men who perished in the Jutland naval battle. The Rev. J. M. Buckmaster, alluding to the crowning grief of the passing of Lord Kitchener, following upon the shock the nation had sus- tained by the loss of 5,000 lives of the very best men possessed by this country, said that some- times convention made it appear that the nation was in grief at a great man's death, but that impression was not always quite sincere. The sorrow professed for the death of Lord Kitchener was not a matter of convention. What man would not have given his own life to save him? Lord Kitchener was no mere figurehead; he meant very much to us all. He was a personal friend; we all knew and loved him. He was so great, so noble, so strong and Ao straight. He was his straightness that we loved. He-never feared to speak the truth. He was the greatest type of an English gentleman; he was a man.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
REV. T. T. JAMES' EULOGY.

The Final Sacrifice Of A Tireless Patriot.

The Rev. T. T. James, preaching at, Wycliffe Church on Sunday morning, made the following touching reference to the late Lord Kitchener: We meet/this morning with the sense that m have suffered during this week a great national loss. The present days are prolific in surprises and in tragedies, and perhaps we had thought ourselves proof against any news that might be brought us. We have as a nation been deeply moved by many of the events of the last twenty months, but I believe it is true to say that -.ve have never during this period been more deeply moved than by the death of Lord Kitchener at sea on Tuesday last. The suddenness and seriousness of the news combined to make it a heavy, shock. I shall content myself this morn- ing by saying that it would be difficult to over- estimate the services which have been rendered to this country in the pre;ent crisis by the late Secretary for War. The existence of a vast army is outward and visible proof enough of the fruits of his labour, his skill in organisation, and his tireless industry. Beyond that it 4s im- possible to state what he meant to this countr)r in the confidence which he inspired, in the calm- ness and coolness with which he confronted the gravest problems, in the self-control and dignity with which he could face a crisis and turn it to advantage. One other ,thing I will say: I be- lieve it is difficult to say too much of the way in which his absolute devotion to duty, and his frank and unselfish disposition, won the affection and esteem of those who met him in the Councils of State, and also in the ranks of the Army itself.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
Grateful Free Churchmen.

As Free Churchmen we had every reason to be grateful for the constant courtesy and con- sideration with which he treated the just claims of our Church, and as Christian men and women we have reason to be thankful that he knew the tremendous value of religious faith. The lines well befit him which were first penned in Praise of a great predecessor in the history of our land

He is gone who seemed so great,— Gone; but nothing can bereave him Of the force he made his own Being here, and we believe him Something far advanced in State, And that he wears a truer crown Than any wreath that man can weave him.

We want to finish this conflict well. We want to see it through so that there shall be a better world for our children, and our children's chil- dren, a world where God is not merely talked about, but where he is honoured and obeyed. We are very far from being discouraged or .le.. pressed. There is a new thrill of hope within us and a new determination because we have seen one of the greatest and most distinguished men that we possessed made one with the simplest and humblest lad who ever hela rifle in the trench, and the lowliest and poorest bluejacket who ever trod a deck, in that final sacrifice in which a man lays down his life for his friends.

The special music included the singing of "Nearer, my God to Thee" and the playing of the Dead March in " Saul" by the .organist, Mr. W. IL Pa3iton. '

TRIUMPH AND TRAGEDY.

Rev. E. Downham On Kitchener And

The Promised Land. • A ••most impressive and largely attended Memorial service was held at St. Paul's Church on Sunday morning. The St. Paul's and St. Elphin's Church Lads' Brigade were in attend- ance, 100 strong, under the command of Major J. Brookes, Captain F. Bunch and Lieutenant N. Morris. There were also present Captain. Mathams, officer commanding the administra- tive centre 4th South Lancashire Regiment, and a number of wounded soldiers from the White. cross Hospital, of which the Rev. E. Downham is the chaplain. The drums were massed before the altar and covered With the Union Jack. Among the hymns that were sung were Kipling's Recessional and "For all the Saints who from their labours rest." After the Blessing, "The Last Post" was sounded with brilliant effect from the belfry by Sergeant IL S. Goodwin, a former C.L.B. lad, who has been on active ser- vice with the Territorials; and the service con- cluded with the singing of the National Anthem in its entirety. From the tower of the church the flag flew at half-mast, and the bells rang a muffled peal. The offertory, who realised £4 Ss. 7d., was in aid of the fund for blinded soldiers. A Comparison With Moses.

The Rev. E. Downham, -who preached from Joshua, i., 2, "Moses my servant is dead! now, therefore, arise, go over this Jordan," said Moses is dead." There is a tragedy in these three words. Here is a man who, under the guidance of God, trained the manhood of the Hebrews for the cause of freedom and national existence against the tyranny and oppression of Egypt. Here is a man who, having led the children of Israel through the wilderness,, had slain mighty kings and overthrown ancient nations. Moses had only to, cross Jordan and complete the conquest. Then—even then on the eve of the triumph—the tragedy came: "Moses is dead. In this old-world story we have the situation to-day. "Kitchener is dead." What a tragedy there is in these three words! That towering personality, that mighty spirit has passed forth from this scene of action to the great universe beyond. Here is a man whose power and wisdom have been made known in the lands of mighty kings and ancient peoples. Here is a man at whose summons the greatest army of free and willing men in the history of the world has come into being. His name worked like magic in the world. It inspired his countrymen; it breathed confidence into the councils of the Allies; it struck terror in the camp of the enemy. And now, behold the tragedy has come. He is taken from us before that great army of victory Which he had created had com Meted its work. Yet he had no doubt as to the end. He has passed from us, and only from a distance was he permitted to view the promised land of victory.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
Let Us Catch the Spirit of This Hero.

To-day we thank God for the gift of this great man. We thank Him that He has de- livered him out of many dangers, and spared him to us so long. We thank Him for all that the great Field-Marshal has achieved for this Empire and its people. We thank Him that He has guided him in the building up of our great army, which we believe, will carry on this war of freedom and righteousness to a victorious and glorious end. "Kitchener is dead; now, therefore, arise, go forward." Here is the challenge; here is the clarion call. We recognise our loss, but we must not falter. The cause of God and of humanity must go forward. The very fact that this great man has passed from us is a call to every man and woman to throw themselves into the national cause. Let us catch the spirit o. this silent, this untiring, this indomitable hero, whose loss we mourn, and whose name we revere. In this way, and I believe in this way only, this tragic death shall not be in vain in the Lord. Let us continue with patience and steadfast.. ness the struggle that is set before us, looking unto God, our help and defence. Aye, and let us

Remember him who led your hosts; He bade you guard the sacred coasts. His voice is silent in your council hall vor over; and whatever tempest low, For ever silent; yet remember all He spoke among you, and the Man who spoke.

Remember- this great soul, and when you re. member all he spoke, fail not to recall his words to our Army When he first sent them forth: . Pe your duty bravely. Fear Gad. Honour the King.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR TEMPERANCE AND PURITY.

Rev. W. T. Berry On Changes Kitchener Wrought.

An eloquent tribute was also uttered by the, Rev. W. T. Derry at the Bewsey-road. Wesleytin Church on Sunday evening. It was followed by Mr. Tanner playing on the organ the Dead March in "Saul." The reverend gentleman described Lord Kitchener as being a man of perfect loyalty and utter devotion to duty. Nothing could turn him one hair's breadth from what he saw to be the path of his duty. He was a man of cease- less diligence in performing it, and gave most scrupulous attention to detail in order that his work might be completed. At the same time he had splendid modesty.. He never tried to get into the public eye, never sought advertise- ment, and was never anxious for applause. They knew how interested he was in the temperance of the men under his control. When he left the Army in India that Army was more sober than any similar body of working men in this country, and they knew what temptations the Army had to bear. Not content with that he was anxious for the purity of our soldiers, and before he left India great changes had taken place for the better in this respect. He was always glad of good news of spiritual work amongst the soldiers, realising that the fittest man was a man whose life was best lived, and whose life was given to God, even as his own was. It had been given Lord Kitchener to set his mark on India, Egypt, and South Africa, and now his name was emblazoned across Europe. The news of his tragic end had upset us all, but we should be grateful that the work of organisation Lord Kitchener did was practi- cally completed before the end came.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
A GREAT NATIONAL CREATOR.

Glowing Tribute At Cairo Street Memorial Service.

The special memorial service in Cairo-streot Chapel on Sunday morning was largely attended, and the congregation heard an eloquent and impressive sermon by the Rev. Irvine Lister, on the life of Lord Kitchener. _ .

_ For nearly two years during the most anxious period in our national life, he said, Lord Kitchener had been one of the leaders to whom we looked with unabated confidence to deliver us victoriously from our present struggle. We had reposed our hopes in him, we had given to him great power and authority, we had trusted in his tremendous gifts. He had had the entire confidence of the whole Empire, and in the fate- ful hour we united to confess that he had more than justified our expectations. When the awful calamity of war descended upon us, no one could foresee what terrible trials we should have to endure, and no one could tell us what malign spirit the war might let loose upon the whole fabric of our civilisation. We realised indeed that much evil might be done to all those institutions we had so dearly won in the past, that truth, freedom, religion, and all the higher things of the spirit were in jeopardy. We felt that the gates of hatred and vice were being opened upon us and that all high and lofty ideals might be swept away. We were drawn into a conflict which might easily have destroyed not only material property, and power, but also our moral and religious ideals. War was a ruthless spirit burning up every- thing gracious, kindly, and brotherly. At that time it was a gigantic burden to place on the r shoulders of any man to prepare us physically to take our honourable part in this vast struggle, and yet at the same time to preserve to us our moral and religious idealism. But not only had he done all these great things for his own people, he had also uplifted us in the eyes .of 'the whole world. He had created respect for us abroad, he had shown to men of other countries what our great Empire stood for and was capable of, and he had bound us in friendship to our Allies as no other man could have done. His Spirit Inspires Us.

tesman had • been This great soldier and sta suddenly removed from his work before it was wholly accomplished, and before he could actually see the fruits of his labour. It was a hard fact for us to realise, and with our limited knowledge' we could not help but regret it. It would have been but right, that, having led us through all the tiresome and responsible work of preparation, he should have also led us on to final victory, but that was denied. He still remained, however, and must ever remain an inspiration to us, and in spirit he would still

Continued in next column.

lead and encourage. The work of such a great national creator could never be forgottln perish, and during the months to come it %could still be his hand that guided and his mind that would direct us towards a consummation. Though he had gone from us, his personality would live, and in the hearts and minds of the citizens of the Empire would still accomplish great tasks. Through a long and strenuous life he served his country with all his great powers and gifts. In all parts of the world he had stood as the representative of our country, and wherever duty had called him he had given unstinted service in order to glorify his nation. He had been the inspirer of law and order. As a soldier and statesman he had always worked for the highest he had known and conceived, and he had left for us a great monument of industry which would ever speak to , eucceeding genera- tions of integrity, patriotism and stern duty with the highest service.

Chief's Appeal for Specials. ,It will be seen from our advertisement columns that great demands have been made by the Army upon the men of the Warrington Police Force, and there is urgent need for more specials to fill the places of those who have answered the call: There are about eighty specials :doing duty at the present time, and we have reason to know that their services are looked upon by the authorities as of real value and that they Are accordingly appreciated. All that is asked of Volunteers is that they shall devote, say, three hours on each of two days a week to police work, which, owing to the present peaceable condition of the town, is not of a very exacting character. There is plenty of responsible duty to be performed. The Chief Constable or the Inspector of Special Constables will be pleased to give any information at the Police Station any evening to those willing to do this patriotic service by their country.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
Responsibilities of the Christian Church.

The Rev. T. T. James, speaking from per- sonal experience at the Front and at home, has become "more and more impressed with the vital nature of the present moment for the Church of Jesus Christ." He holds that it is absolutely necessary that we should realise this not only as individuals but in a collective capacity and as a Christian community. "It is impossible," he says, "to exaggerate the responsi- bility which rests upon the Christian Church to-day. Day by day it grows clearer to me that our only hope of making any contribution to our national life in the present and the future lies in our facing the immediate situation as an unexampled opporunity." He has accord- ingly arranged to preach a series of sermons at Wycliffe on the next six Sunday evenings. The series will be:—" Church Worship and the Time of Crisis," "The Church and the Trenches," "The Church and the Coming Problems," "The Church and the Churchless Masses," "The Church, the War, and the Young," "The Church and the Welcome Home."

, No Noon Delivery. Owing to a further depletion of the Post Office staff in connection with the group system it has been found necessary to abolish the noon delivery, thus reducing the number of deliv- eries to two daily, viz., the first at 7 o'clock a.m., and the second at 3.20 p.m. The Postmaster would like again to call the attention of the public to the earlier closing at 7 p.m. of the Head Office and would be glad if all parcels for dispatch by the night mail could be handed in as early as possible, at least not later than 6 o'clock p.m.

Death of Mr. W. Tomkinson.

We regret to record the death on Friday last of Mr. W. Tomkinson, of Orford-lane, an old resident, who came to the town forty years ago. at the same time as Alderman Bur- ton. He was engaged in the plate mills atti Messrs. Pearson and, Knowles, and along .with the Burton brothers, became associated with the Buckley-street Church, and subsequently, on going to Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co., he joined the Wesleyan Church et: Sankey Bridges. Removing to Birchall-street, he commenced to worship at Brick-street Mission, at which church he played the organ for twenty years, also taking part in many ways in the work of the church and school. The funeral took place on Monday, the remains being conveyed to the Mission, 'where the first part of the interment service was gone through. Subsequently the body was buried at the Ceme- tery in the presence of a large circle of friends.

Tomkinson was well-known in the town, and was a staunch member of the Liberal Committee in St John's Ward.

Prodigious Output of Leather.

The prodigious amount of leather that is being sent out from this district, writes the Warrington correspondent of "The Leather Trades Review," reflects great credit on the town and district. It is playing an important part in the prosecution of the war. Everf works is at full pressure. The workers have smilingly foregone their usual holiday this Whitsuntide, and when one realises the great value of leather to our Forces and those of our Allies, it goes to show that we are undoubtedlY fulfilling a great need very handsomely. The Government have intimated that they will re- quire all the leather- suitable for their purposes that they can get hold of up to the end of September, but in the meanwhile matters are in abeyance as regards prices. Tanners who have been selling little odd lines for the civilian trade have now been informed that if these are of the required substance. as previously stipulated, and unless the inspector has previously rejected them, samples must be submitted to the department for acceptance or refusal. This is, therefore, another blow to the civilian trade, though up to now they appear to have got on very well with light leather.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
PROTECTING CHILDREN.

Co-operative Congress Objects To Them On Farms.

MR. UPSON LEADS THE WAY.

On the initiative of Mr. John Upson, chair- man of the Warrington Co-operative Society, a strong protest against the employment of chil- dren of school age was made at Tuesday's sit- ting of the Co-operative Congress at Lancaster. Two thousand delegates, representing over 3,000,000 members, were present, and only three showed hands against the motion, which ran:— " That this Congress protests against the employment of children of school age in agri- cultural and industrial districts, and demands (a) the raising of the school age at once to an irreducible minimum of 14 for all children, , (b) the further raising of the school age to 15 at the close of the war, and (c), as a necessary corollary, free secondary education for all." Mr. Upson said the delegates would notice by the report of the Central Board of the Societies that a deputation had waited upon Sir John 'Simon and Mr. Arthur Henderson in respect to the emplciyment of children of school age upon the land. Their protest was received with sym- Pathy, but sympathy was not enough. (Hear, hear.) Eight thousand children under school age were already employed on January 21st of this year. Since that time the numbers had been considerably increased, and there was also a growing demand in the industrial districts for the same facilities to be granted as in the agricultural districts. One of the noblest and taost..persistent of the motives of the Co-opera- tive pioneers was a passionate sympathy with the victims of the industrial revolution, especi- ally with the women and children. The Factory Act of 1819, limiting to twelve hours per day the labour of children in mills, Was almost entirely the work of Robert Owen and us friends. Since that time a protective barrier, ec)nsisting of the Education Acts and a complex code of factory legislation, had been erected about the child. That barrier was in danger of being broken down. Let it not be forgotten that It was the workers' child who was in danger. (Applause.) It always was so, and always would be until organised democracy said, once and for all, that the immature bodies of little children should not be the remedy for economic, social, or national ills.

Industry Must Do Without The Young. "I need not dwell upon the old arguments as to the need of the child's wage to maintain the home, or the poor widow argument," he continued. "The new afguments are more sPecious, although coming from the same sus- Picious quarters as the old: they are those CI the national need. It is said that, owing to the Shortage of labour, industry, particularly agri- culture, cannot possibly be carried on without Tecruiting children of school age to the ranks of labour. If it can be shown that the country's aeed is so great that the existence of the State 48 imperilled, that every alternative has been tried, and nothing remains but to take children from the schools, then we must swallow this hitter pill of hideous war. But I cannot for one taotnent admit that we have, yet reached that Point of national peril. (Applause.) To do so Would be living upon our capital with a vengeance. Lost wealth may be recreated, lost Pleasure, comfort, luxury, will be regained; but !fie national loss in the child is irreparable. It is said that this expedient is only for the Period of the war, but if we allow this we are creating precedents, and our example will be quoted as an unanswerable- argument in all Yuture periods of shortage of labour." (Hear, hear.)

Attack was ever the best method of defence; hence the demand made in the resolution. They were asked by others to wait until the war was Over, but if they did so it would be too late. A hnndred and one questions would be then Vailnant for settlement, and this matter would bef„indefinitely shelved. Before the war we had dePendent upon Germany for many things. at dependence would not be destroyed "oy In uoYcot „" ts. tariffs, isolating Germany, or federat- th" Ilnpire. It would only be destroyed as whole youth of the nation was trained and developed in its mental and moral capacities. 110 doubt it woUld be said. that tti raise the Echool ago now ,would be impossible. That was said when it was proposed to raise the age from nine to ten years, and it had always been said When attempts had been made to improve the lot of the worker and his child.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
Free Secondary Education For An.

The need of raising the age limit would be understood when they remembered that there were six millions of children in our elementary schools, one-half of whom never reached the highest class. The greatest regret about this was that it was in the highest class that the most fruitful educational work of the element- -42? schools was done. "Free secondary educa- -lion for all !" demanded Mr. Upson,, and asked "Why not? If it is essential for the children 'of the well-to-do, it is equally essential for the children of the poor. It is the duty of the Btate to supply everyone of its youthful citizens, 441 entering'on life's pilgrimage, with an ade- quate educational equipment to ensure that ftCh has the opportunity to train and develop Powers of mind and soul to the limit of his caPacity. How far we are from that will be !calised when we remember that in our elemen- taly schools there are six millions or scholars, °lid in 1913 there were only 150,000 in secondary *'ehools of all kinds." „Ur- Upson made an earnest appeal that the '°ngress, in a very true sense the mouthpiece 2f democracy, should give the child a wider, a;linier, outlook on life; let him know that (-Ws mind to him a. kingdom is"; that in the ftereise of his brain power, his accumulations °f knowledge, and his independency of soul, he Vis more fully fulfilling the divine purpose of is being than he was in gathering material Pc_ ssessions. "Give him a full life," the 7Leeaker concluded earnestly, "a mind rich in knowledge of history, literature and science, Soul trained to self-restraint, to a sense of duty 11(1 responsibility, and he will see to it that rither he nor his fellows shall live a life but ditttle above that of the beasts that perish." ."-lcud applause.)

NEW JUSTICES,

Mr. Holman Kingdon A J.P.

For Cheshire.

Friday last week- it was announced that the following had been placed on the commission of the peace for the county of Chester :—Mr. John Brotherton, of Elm Cottages., Helsby; Mr. G. H. K. Kingdon, of Hill Crest, Frodsham; Mr. F. W. Wignall, of The Rockery, Tattenhall ; and Mr. Thomas Wright, of The Poplars, Frod- sham.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. KINGDON.

Although Mr. George Holman Kent Kingdon, whose name figures in the list of new magis- trates for the County of Chester, resides at Frodsham, his business life lies in Warrington, where he is well known as a clear-sighted, sound-judging man of affairs. He was educated at Oxford, where he graduated in 1894, taking an Honours. Degree in Natural Science. He is a managing director of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., of Warrington,' and William Gossage and Sons, Ltd., of Widnes, and has

===== 0002601_19160617_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. HOLMAN KINGDON.

been associated with the former firm during a period when very rapid and extensive progress has been made. Practical Educationist.

He is a thorough believer in giving the people the best possible education, and as a .Member of the Education Committee for the past twelve years he has lost no opportunity of demonstrat- ing how closely the welfare of the nation and of the Empire is bound up with the proper mental training of the workers. The health of the children, too, has claimed his earnest attention, ,and he hailed with the delight of a practical reformer the chance that Parliament gave to Education 'Authorities, a few years ago, of seeing that children should ,be made physi- cally fit to absorb the mental food with which the schools are now supplying them. Six years ago he was made chairman of the Medical In- spection Sub-Committee, which, under his guidance, placed Warrington in possession of one of the earliest school treatment and dental clinics in the country. Much good has already been. done in the way of detecting physical defects in children, effacing them, and checking their evil effects, judicious treatment on the part of the officials, who, under the committee, administer the Acts, having gradually obtained the helpful co-operation of all parents who have the best interests of their children at heart.

That Mr. Kingdon is not 'inclined to trifle with his share in public life was recently de- monstrated by the characteristic attitude he assumed in a dispute with the Health Com- mittee of the Corporation: That committee claimed, and in fact asserted, the right to appoint all the officials Of the Education Com- mittee on the medical side, although the Edu- cation Committee had largely to pay their salaries and to direct their labours. Mr. King- don felt, and rightly felt, that a position in which he and his colleagues were to be respon- sible for and pay men and women whose appointment rested with others, was untenable; and having tried for a long time, and in vain, to come to some rational understanding with the Health Committee, he refused to proceed and resigned from the Medical Inspection Com- mittee. Other members of the Committee who had been jointly . responsible for putting the Act into 'operation resigned with him and the medical work of the committee came to a stand. In this state of deadlock the. Government De- partment was appealed to, and we now learn that the Health Committee have agreed to allow the Medical Inspection Committee a voice in the appointments to which they have claimed a right. At the last annual meeting of the Education Committee Mr. Kingdon and his protesting colleagues allowed themselves to be replaced upon the committee, which was taken as a sign that their demands had been granted.

A Busy Man's Interests.

Mr. Kingdon is an active member of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce, of which he is an ex-president. He was one of the late Mr. Willett's most ardent admirers, and long before Germany forced us into it he advocated the adoption of daylight saving in speeches before the Chamber, which fell in the main upon deaf ears. The new magistrate is a member of the Inner Temple, a Fellow of the Chemical Society, and a Freemason. He is a very busy man, but he finds time to be secretary of the Frodsham Military Hospital, and a Platoon Commander in the Frodsham Volunteer Corps. He is a member of the Board of Trade Advisory Com- mittee for Liverpool ankdistrict, and formerly represented Warrington on the Advisory Com- mittee of Faculty of Commerce of Victoria University, Manchester.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 179 1916.

1 HE "VOICE" OF LABOUR.

Workers' National League And The Trades Council.

FACING THE FACTS.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—l have read the report of the Trades Council meeting in your issue of June the 10th re the question Of nominees for the War Pen- sions Committee, and see that exception was taken by the Trades Council to the British Workers' National League (Warrington Branch) nominating candidates for the said committee.

The reason given was that we were a poli- tical organisation. Why that should debar us from nominating I am yet unable to under- stand, even if the statement was true that we were a political organisation, which. is an incorrect statement A manifesto issued from the head office of the union stated that it was non-political and invited anyone, irrespective of political opinion. The Warrington Branch already consists of members who have different party views. But I should like to ask the question: What are Messrs. Dukes and Seabury if they are not , politicians? Are not the Town Council object- ing to them acting on the committee not because they are trade unionists, but because of their political action in this crisis of our country? In case it should interest any trade unionists or others to know why the League has formed a branch in Warrington, I may point out that the Warrington Trades 'Council are misrepre- senting the spirit of the majority of trade unionists in Warrington. However much we as a country are to blame in regard to this war, we are in it, and we have got to get out of it at the least cost of life to our fellow country- men. Whilst there are those of our kith and kin suffering and dying in the trenches, we are going to give them all the support and encour- agement we can, as we feel sure that we shall be reflecting the temper and spirit of the majority of trade unionists at home, to say nothing of those who are fighting for their country. We have heard of late a great deal about who represents the voice of Labour in Warrington. Up to now we have nothing to prove it.

With all due respect to the majority of the Trades Council officials, they know full well that they do not represent the majority of the trade unionists in regard to this war, not even those left at home. But 'for argument's sake, we'll say they do; but what about those who have left these shores (rightly or wrongly) on purpose to defend this country from the cruel invaders? Should their spirit and opinions be taken no account of? I think all fair-minded trade unionists would agree that was an unfair proposal. Speaking for the League I should like to in- form all and sundry, including Warrington Trades Council,. that we are not in the pocket of either Councillor Roberts or any other employer of labour who has been indirectly referred to, but we still stand true to the class to which we belong and to do our uttermost to help those who cannot help themselves. Thanking you in anticipation that you will favour me with inserting this itn your next issue.

Yours faithfully. C. WOZENCROFT. Fiddlers' Ferry, Warrington.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
ROYAL NAVAL RESERVE.

Lieutenant Who Fought In Belgium Makes Appeal.

The appeal for volunteers for the Royal Naval Reserve which Sub-Lieutenant Mapson has been making at the Royal Court Theatre, the Palace and Hippodrome and various picture houses this week, has been impressive and stirring, and par- ticularly valuable because the recruiter himself could wain his hearers of the fate that is in store for us should G'erniuny break thrcugh and invade us.

Mr. Mapson has seen thesó horrors himself. "Some of 'us saw a little of the kind of work they were doing in Belgium at the beginning of the war," he said. "I saw little children who had had their hands and feet cut off, and I saw old men and women who had been bayoneted and left against fences. The same fate would have overtaken you in Britain if it were not for the fact that ever driving the enemy back on land you have our gallant lads in khaki, and ever holding the freedom of the seas you have the lads in navy blue." (Applause.) The speaker pointed out that it was owing to the fact that we had held command of the seas during these 22 months of war that they were able to sit securely in theatres, safe from in- vasion and fearing no attack, because between them and the hordes of would-be oppressors, there was ever stretched out the strong- arm of the British Navy.

Recently., as they all knew, the Navy had just been depleted by 7.000 brave men, and he honed that courageous fellows would come forward to take their places. Those who had attested under Lord Derby's scheme and had not yet been apportioned to a regiment were at liberty to be transferred to the Naval Reserve. By join- ing they would help to insure that Britannia should continue to rule the waves. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160617_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
PEACE AND WAR.

A Reverie.

Within the west the eve has set Its seal upon the summer night: I linger in the waning light, But cannot see the stars as yet.

For me no ray of Hope is shed Along the pathways of the sky. But through the shadows seem to fly The silent armies of the dead.

For when the sunset fell on me, It seemed to flow, a lurid flood Of shadow from the land of blood Across the hills, ,across the sea.

Men have to reap what they have sown, But sad it is this latter day Needs look on warring hosts, and they Each other's brothers, and our own.

0 gallant sons! not yours the blame! Ye met the foe without a fear : Ye held the ,eause of Freedom dear, And bore her flag through flood and flame

But brothers, will it have no end, This hell you hold on earth above? Look up upon the star of Love And learn to call thy brother friend.

In all the passion and the pain, The shock of arms, the overthrow, The outer and the inner woe I cannot see as yet the gain.

Nay, but I count it bitter loss, The flower of English manhood slain! s, The dead we loved come not again, Though wives and mothers kiss their cross.

I know. that watching every scene From hour to hour, from first to last, The present sits beside the past, And shapes its form from what has been.

For in this stern, relentless hate, Such things are done from day to day As tears will never weep away, Nor after-anguish compensitte.

What Demon leads the German bands? What hellish shadow them allures? Immortal Freedom still endures, Though smitten by the tyrants' hands.

0 Motherland so blest in peace, So fair withal by field and shore, Peace is the blessing evermore That only gives a land increase.

Come Peace beloved! for you I pray, The chosen daughter of the Lord! Bid warring nations sheathe the sword; And usher in a brighter day. ALFRED CORNET.

Charity may begin at home, but it is fre- quently out when. anyone calla.

LOST IN THE GREAT SEA FIGHT.

FRIENDS GO DOWN.

Gunner Yates 8c Stkr. Dutton's Fate On The Defence.

On a wall in the kitchen of 9, Cockhedge-lane, are the photographs ef a quartet of chums whose deaths are now mourned by Mr. and Mrs. William Yates and family, the occupants of the house. One is a likeness of ' their son and brother, First Class Gunner Alexander James Yates; another of Stoker T. H. Dutton of Hatton, both of whom went down with H.M.S. Defence in the recent battle; another is Able- bodied Seaman George Hargreaves, of Howley, who yielded up his life with the Monmouth off the Chilean Coast; the last is of Private George Henry Woodey, of the K.R.R., who fell at Neuve Chapelle just over twelve months ago. Only a fortnight before the naval victory in which they lost their lives, Yates and Dutton were home on leave, both joyful in each other's company and homes. They went back full of hope and eagerness for a fight with the Ger- mans, their chief song being "A life on the ocean wave," a song which admirably sets to

metre the high spirits of our men of the sea. Gunner Yates had not reached his twentieth birthday, but measured six feet in hisstockings and had three years' experience on men-of-war. He had adopted the sea as his calling; he loved it and intended serving his twenty-one years. His rapid progress gave him encouragement, for he was recognised as a First Class ,Gunner, and we may be sure that ere the Defence be- came immersed in the -tossing waters his shots found sensitive billets in the proud and vain ships of the enemy. The Jutland fight was not his first experience of sea warfare, for he was in at the Falkland Islands affair, when Arbuthnot silenced Von Spree and avenged Craddock. At the time the order was received for action against' Von Spree the Defence was cruising in the Mediterranean and raced 5,000 miles across the Atlantic, being specially mentioned in dispatches for her valiant part in the action which destroyed all German hopes of big raids on Allied commerce. Guard of Honour to King and Queen of Albania.

During a Balkan rising, when the King and Queen of "Albania sought refuge on the Defence, Yates formed -one of the Guard of Honour, sending homez.ta description of the scene—the King was Wearing a blue Coat, with a collar of white fur, ant the Queen was attired in pure white. silk. "Moth looked scared and .glad to get on board- Cur ship," he said. • Then he described his bwn clothes. Soon after their Balkan Majesties had embarked there was a fusillade of bullets from the shore, and his Guard_ of Honour clothing got torn in the sleeves, chest and trousers. He had the nar- rowest of escapes from death. Gunner Yates first went to Wycliffe School, I3ewsey‘street, and, subsequently, to St. James' (Latchford), leaving there to work at Cock- hedge Mills. Before joining the Defence he served on H.M.S. Powerful and H.M.S. Impreg- nable. When home on leave he always visited ,his. old chapel in Buckley-street, which his parents also attend. . His father is patriotically-minded, too. He was in the Volunteers for over five years and last June enlisted in the South Lancashire Regi=, ment, but -was discharged in, February. • He' is now strenuously engaged in producing muni- tions, leaving home for Manchester every morn- ing at 6.50 and returning at 9.30. •

STOKER DUTTON'S CAREER.

First-Class Stoker Thomas Henry Dutton, lost on H.M.S. Defence, was a son of Mr. and Mrs. R. Dutton, of New Cottages, Hatton, near War-

FIRST-CLASS STOKER T. IL DUTTON. Lost on H.M.S. Defence:

rington. He was 21 years of age, and as a boy attended Daresbury School. He was formerly a member of the Halton and Daresbury Church choirs. He joined the Navy in March, 1913, having previously been employed by Mr. A. Broome, farmer, Hallam Hall, Newton-by- Daresbury, and later at the Grappenhall Tube Works. ON HONOUR'S SCROLL.

WOUNDED.

PRIVATE E. HOWARD.

Missing Since June Last; Now Local Boxer Wounded In The Lungs. Reported Dead. Private Edward Howard, of the itoyal Welsh Fusiliers, son of Mr. and Mrs. T. Howard, of f 6, Brierley-street, is lying in the Edinburgh War Hospital suffering from u serious wound, but he is" progressing favourably. Writing on Wednesday, he said I am lucky to be here, for all my pals in the platoon were killed alongside of me, and when I got hit I thought my last days had come. I managed, however, to crawl back to' the trench. The shrapnel went in near My shoulder-blade, and hit my lungs, and then stuck in my ribs. I could not speak for a day or two after it was done. I expect to be under- going an operation shortly." Private Howard, who is 19 years of age, en- listed in September. He had been previously employed as a bottler by Messrs. Eddowes, mineral water manufacturers. He was edu-

Taylor, 27, Leicester- street, the War Office has concluded that he was killed or died of wounds on June 16th last year. Since the big engagement on that date Private Taylor had been reported wounded and missing, and nothing further had been .heard until a few weeks ago. Before enlisting Private Taylor, who was 23 years of age, was employed by his uncle, Mr. T. Higham, pork butcher, Green-street. REPORTED KILLED.

PRIVATE R. TAYLOR.

LORD KITCHENER'S RELIGIOUS FAITH.

The well-known Church paper the "Guar- dian" publishes the following : " Lord Kitchener was so reticent a man and (save to a few intimates) so little was known of his private life that the statements we print to-day of his attitude towards religion will be read with peculiar interest. He was many years a member of the English Church Union, and was a member to the end. of that Guild of the Holy Standard which performs so admirable a work among soldiers and their families. "Distinguished commanders have nearly always been men of religion, and Lord prac- tical form, and his old interest, Biblical archaso- Kitchener's faith took a very concrete and logy, as evidenced by his work for the Palestine Exploration Fund, may very well have attuned his thoughts to the deeper verities that were first revealed amid the famous scenes with which he was once øo Peculiarly

===== 0002601_19160617_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
KILLED.

PRIVATE G. PLUMPTON.

Hit By Rifle Bullet And Passes Away Peacefully.

Official news has reached Mrs. Plulapton, 25, Crossley-steeet, that her husband. Private George Plumpton, of the South Lancashire Regiment, was killed in action on June Bth. The following letter, dated June Bth, has been received from Captain C. Collingwood It is with the very greatest regret that I take upon myself the sad duty of informing you that your husband has fallen in the field of honour. He was hit by a rifle bullet in the early hours of this morning and passed away peacefully with- out regaining consciousness. The bullet pene- trated to the region of the heart, and it will be some consolation to you to s know that he suffered no pain at all. Every man in the com- pany is saddened by this catastrophe, for your husband was well liked and universally admired for his many excellent qualities. Dear Madam, you have my most heartfelt sympathy in your trouble, and for your comfort I would like,- if I may, to point out that your husband has died the death of a gallant gentleman, passing in a moment from a field of glorious self-sacrifice and devotion to a realm more glorious still. May God help and comfort you in your great grief." Private Plumpton, who was 28 years of age, was formerly employed by Messrs. Rylands Bros., Ltd:, and has often played for the works' team in the football competitions. He was educated at the National School, Church-street. He had been on active service almost twelve months, and leaves a widow and one child.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
MISSiNG.

TROOPER F. J. STONE. Uncertain News As To His Fate.-

Unofficial news has reached Mr. C. Garton Stone, head brewer to Messrs. Greenall, Whit- ley and Co., Ltd., that his brbther, Trooper F. J. Stone, of the Canadian Mounted Rifles, has been missing sinee the recent severe fighting near Ypres. This news was sent by a comrade, but no official news has yet been received. Trooper Stone, who is 34 years of age, is one of three soldier-brothers, all of whom are serv- ing at the Front. He went to Canada six or seven years ago, where he was engaged in farming. He enlisted about a fortnight after the outbreak of war, and before leaving Eng- land with his regiment he spent a short leave with his brother at Warrington. .

RAIDED ENEMY'S RATIONS.

Warrington Man's Successful Hunt

For Food.

" Boar Editor," writes Lance-Corporal Taylor, of the Lancashire Fusiliers, "Being a constant reader of your valuable paper, which I find very useful in the trenches, I would like you to insert a few experiences of one of our town lads in the Lancashire Fusiliers, named Andrew Clancy, better known among his comrades as the' White Ghurka.'

"For. instance, at the bluff, in the recapture, we could not get rations for three days. We all spoke to the White Ghurka. "Leave it to me, boys; it has almost broken my heart these last two days to see, what you have gone through,' and off he went under heavy machine gun and rifle fire with nothing only a few bombs. We had almost given him up when we heard a ringing cheer on our left. We gripped our rifles, but to our great delight we saw it was the White Ghurka returning with something on his back. "Just fancy our joy when we found that he had captured two loaves of black bread and a sack containing flour and jam from the baby killers, and what was more be fetched a brown cat, which is now called the Lancashire Fusi- liers' mascot. Our town's lad was soon on the job with the flour and jam. He had a fire burn. ing before we could say Jacky Fish,' and we did very well with jam rolls until our rations came up the following night. This is only one of his many experiences since he came out here. He has been recommended by C.-S.-31. sl,&rgt. Bruff and .31.-C, McDeirtnpAnt.'"

===== 0002601_19160617_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Jerry-building. The appearance of "Jerry and Co., btrfiders," at the Hippodrome this week reminded me of an old " wheeze " that I have been inithe habit of trotting out—if I mix not my metaphors—on occasion over a considerable period. Two jerry- builders erected semi-detached villas with the intention of occupying them, but just before , they were fit for habitation, the rains descended and the floods came and beat upon those houses, and one of them fell. Next morning the respective owners went together to ascertain the extent of the damage. Contemplating with rueful countenance the ruins of his handiwork, the heavy loser said to his fellow, "However is't 'at mine's blown down and thine hasn't?" "Why, tha sees, was the reply, "I'd getten mine papered!" Coffee Unadulterated.

The Deputy-Mayor (Dr. Joseph) comments with the utmost satisfaction (and I am proud to be privileged to echo his praise) upon the point in the analyst's report as to an entire absence of adulteration in the borough so far as coffee is concerned, whereas fermerly as much as 50 per cent, of chicory had been found. In a Yorkshire town in , which I lived long ago there was a Quaker who diet a big business in that Tine; but I noticed that when a customer asked for a pound of the best coffee, the man behind the counter invariably asked "Pure coffee?" It always seemed to me that that was implied by the terms of the order, and one was drkwen to the unpleasant inference that a buyer caught off guard might have thrust upon him a mixture at a cost of 2s. which contained a large percentage of chicory worth only a few coppers. The Weather.

The compiler of the " Farm and Garden" column is naturally anxious on this score. I think I have previously mentioned that when I was playing the part of editor, I often received from Londbn predictions, supported by elaborate diagrams, purporting to forecast the weather throughout the ensuing year. Here is an ex- ample of the style of this refreshingly cocksure prophet On under ten hours' sunshine, with a slight westerly breeze. It will be very fine about 3 a.m.' cloudy till 8 a.m., then very -fine generally, with occasionally partially cloudy intervals until after 7 p.m., being overcast between 8 and 9 p.m. until near midnight.' How very precise and definite—if only it had possessed one other merit of supreme importance. Yet the prophet confidently proclaimed his ability to foretell the weather for every day and in any part of the world. WRII I always did admire the colossal. Respecting Rain.

I came across it years ago and quote from memory, but I believe it was Mrs. Humphrey Ward who wrote

" The rain it raineth every day Upon the just and unjust fellows; But more upon the just, because The unjust have the just's umbrellas."

That, however, by the way. The rain patters on my windows as I write, and turns my thoughts to the cause. How is it produced? I read that several distinguished men, including Dalton and Leslie, agree with Dr. Hutton's declaration: "Rain results from the mingling together of great beds of air, of unequal temperatures, differently stored with moisture." The author of "Heat, light and work" claims to have solved the problem for himself 30 years ago, and his More detailed explanation is that "heat is subjeet to .centrifugal force, is thrown to the surface of the earth as the Equator line, warms the atmosphere which ascends, taking up moisture which returns as a wet and warm wind, south-westerly as a rule, dropping out the moisture as rain, the heat entering the earth again to be thrown out again, and so the circle goes on." Adds this authority—" We could not have rain if the air remained the same tempera- ture. The heat must enter the earth or be absorbed by an upper stratum of atmosphere." A Curious Fact.

It is undeniable that rain gauges at different altitudes register altogether differently, the higher one invariably recording less rain than the -lower. This has been clearly established by e±perinaents by Dr. Heberden, and as incon- trovertibly demonstrated by Professor Arago, of the Paris Observatory. These assurances afforded me decided relief. In one of my old districts I was regularly favoured with the re- sults of the rainfall observations at two points in the locality, and I was constantly puzzled by the fact that the report from a station on a hilltop always gave lower figures as compared with the one from a lower level. Since then, as just indicated, enlightenment has come from unquestionable sources. Comfort for the Inconsistent.

To revert for a moment to the strange case of the London weather prophet, I have more than hinted that that cheerful gentleman was a trifle inconsistent. But, bless you, that mat- ters not in the least. Ralph Waldo Emerson himself, dealing with this very matter. with characteristic thoroughness, makes plain that he was not obsessed by this word "inconsistency." He derides it, indeed, as the "hobgoblin of little minds," and, while granting that it is "adored by little statesmen and philosophers and divines," declares that "with consistency a great soul has simply nothing to do." Here is a comforting sentence: "Speak what you think now in hard words, and to-morrow speak what to-morrow thinks in hard words again, though it contradict everything you said to- day. Ab, so shall you be misunderstood." And what then? Pythagoras, Socrates, Jesus, Luther, Copernicus, Galileo, Newton, "and every pure and bright spirit that ever took flesh" suffered misunderstanding. Brevity.

Some time ago I gave an example of old Molike's extreme taciturnity. It is said he never uttered more than 15 words in proposing a toast at a public dinner. Once a man pre- sent on such an occasion made a bet with a friend that the rum old veteran would give in nine words a toast standing in his name, and he would have won if Moltke had not added the word "gentlemen "—making ten in all. "The field-marshal is getting old," was 'the loser's disgusted comment; "he begins to be talkative." After all, in this particular, as in the war, the German is destined to be beaten by the French. When Victor Hugo wished to be informed as to the success of "Les Miser- ables," he simply wrote to the publishers a sign of interrogation—" ? " They replied in like manner " !" and the author was no longer anxious. The notorious Dr. Abernethy was much given to that sort of thing, and once met his match in an injured patient who was shown into his surgery. The whole conversa- tion, opened by the doctor, consisted of— "Bruiser' "Bite." " Cat?" "Dog." Brevity is' the soul of wit, 'tis said. The owner of offices tenanted by a firm of solicitors wrote concerning the approaching expiration of the lease, asking if they were going to continue in occupation, and was a bit scandalised on re- ceipt of the reply: "Dear sir,—We are.—Yours truly." He wrote somewhat warmly, deprecat- ing trifling, and demanding an immediate answer to the plain question; "Are you going to remain in the offices or are you not?" And the jokers answered: "Dear sir,—We remain.— Yours faithfully."

WHAT SOLDIERS DO FOR CORNS, CALLOUSES, BUSTERS, BUNIONS, &c., AND WHEN FEET SWELL, ACHE, BURN, SMART AND PERSPIRE.

This softens even the worst corns, so they pain- lessly come right out root and all at the first touch. On account of distance from the heart serious foot disorders are usually due to defec- tive circulation, shoe pressure and consequent irritation at these extremities. The saltrated water will immediately stimulate the circula- tion, clear out sebaceous and poisonous matter from the clogged pores, render the skin active ami healthy, and prevent offensive odours or injurious effects of acid perspiration. Try this refreshing saltrated foot-bath after coming in footsore from tennis or a long walk. "SLUR FROM WARRINGTON."

Runcorn Rural Guardians Take Umbrage.

A short time ago the Warrington Board of Guardians requested that the Runcorn Rural\ Board should allow a woman living in the War- rington area 10s. instead of ss. per week, but the Runcorn Board declined, and the Warring- ton Guardians referred the matter to the Loeal Government Board. The Clerk (Mr. W. E. Rough) told the mem- bers of his Board, at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday afternoon, that .the Warrington Guardians had now requested particulars as to why the increase had been refused.

'"lt is rather a slur on the part of the War- rington Board," remarked the Chairman (Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun.). "They should have asked us for our reasons before sending the affair to the Local Government Board." (Hear, hear.) The Board unanimously decided to leave the matter in the hands of the Clerk, who intimatad that any information the Local Government Board wanted to enable them to decide the merits of the case would be furnished.

Our Smart Fire Brigade.—Our local fire bri- gade turned out for a practice last Saturday afternoon. Very smart indeed they looked with their two lovely dapple grey horses galloping through the village. The firemen, with Captain W. Rose in command, wearing their polished helmets, and mounted on their up-to-date steamer and hose carriage, with whistles blow- ing, were a credit to our Council. This is their first public appearance under their newly-ap- pointed captain, and the Lymm Urban Council are to be congratulated on their selection. The villagers turned out in large numbers and gave them a rousing reception as they passed along. We can all sleep safely in our beds now with the sure confidence that our local brigade can be counted on promptly responding to any emergency.

Sergt. T. W. Field Home on Sick Furlough.— Sergt. T. W. Field, D.C.M., arrived home this week on a short sick furlough. On our repre- sentative calling at his home at Ngwfields, he found the gallant Sergeant peacefully reclin- ing in his favourite armchair, and taking matters somewhat easy. On asking him if he could walk yet, the Sergeant said, " Walk ! Why, I shall be playing cricket before the month is out, if I continue to make the same progress as I am doing at the Present. The doctors told me that I should never be able to walk again, but they were wrong. I have walked miles with the aid of a stick since I came out of hospital." It will be remembered that Sergt. T. W. Field had a relapse some weeks ago, and was admitted to hospital suffer- ing from paralysis in the legs.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 17, 1916.

THE LATE SERGEANT SAM LEWIS.

(Chester), Miss Baxter, Mr. and Mrs. Leathei and Mrs. Shaw. _

Then came the wounded soldiers from Brook- field, and the two local platoons of Volunteers, under the command of Capt. Hawkins. Other soldiers present were Private William Daniels (friend) and Bomb-Instructor-Sergeant G. E. Copeland, both of the Cheshire Regiment. As the procession was passing The Cross, the Rev. Shirers Gill and about thirty senior scholars of the Congregational Sunday School, to which the late Sergeant belonged, joined in and walked in the rear of the Volunteers. At the Church.

As the cortege reached the church che large crowd that had congregated outside suddenly became completely silent, and as the party entered the church not a sound could be heard save for the occasional sob of a mourner. The coffin was borne into church by four stalwart Volunteers.

A Tribute to Lord Kitchener.

The service in churche was conducted by the Rector (the Rev. G. B. Thurston), assisted by the Rev. Horace Gray: Every. available seat was occupied, some hundreds having failed to gain admittance. In the congregation were Lieutenant Wild (sth Manchester Regiment), and Messrs. R. Oliver, W. N. I:4lplesworth, W. M. Beckett, H. M. Johnson, G: A. Cook and T. F. W. Dixon.

The hymns sung were "Fight the Good Fight," and "Jesu, Lover of my Soul." Mr. W. B. Cooke was at the organ and played the Dead March in "Saul."

The Rector addressed the congregation in a few well-chosen words. We are here, he said, to-day to bury a well-known local lad, who has given his life, indirectly, for his country. We are reminded of a debt we owe to our gallant armies in the field, who are fighting the cause of freedom. Several gallant men from this parish have died heroes' deaths in foreign lands, fighting the cause of right. The loss of Lord Kitchener makes us fully realise that "In the midst of life we are in death." Of Lord Kitch- ener that was startlingly, literaHy true. We salute one who can truthfully be called in the Providence of God the saviour 'bf his country, a chief among the saviours of the liberties of Europe, for he had the presence to see that the freedom of the world was in danger unless we did our part in sending all our available man- hood to the fight. It has seemed good to God to call him to another spliere. We bow our heads and say "God's will be done," fully con- fident that his spirit will remain to animate soldiers and civilians alike to greater acts, if necessary, of self-sacrifice, in order• to bring this, the greatest of all wars, to a successful issue.

A man (40), who appealed on personal grounds, stated that he was a sales manager, and if he had to go he and his wife and children would suffer serious hardship through loss of business. He kept a motor car for business reasons, and he released his driver several months ago.—Are your people in Manchester appealing for you ?—Yes, but I am appealing here on personal grounds.—The Court decided to adjourn C the case pending the regult of the employer's appeal. Certified Occupation.

A manager of a coal wharf was granted con- ditional exemption on the ground that he was in a certified occupation. Widow's Successful Appeal For Workman. A widow, the owner of a shoeing forge, asked for exemption for her only workman, a shoeing smith and wheelwright. He was the only man left to manage the business, and if he went the business would have to close.—How long have you carried on the business yourself P—Two years.—How long did your husband before you? —About seven years.—How long has this man worked for you ?—Ever since my husband died. —Have you plenty of work ?—More than we can really do?—nave you ever advertised for labour? Yes, but of no avail.—What class of work do you generally do P—Mostly agricultural work. She further stated that she had to close a wheel- wright's shop owing to the shortage of labour? —Should you open the wheelwright's business if you could get labour ?—Yes. lam refusing work every day.—The Court decided to grant conditional exemption subject to the man re- maining in his present employment. Builder's Successful Appeal. - - -

------ - A builder and contractor who appealed for conditional exemption stated that if he went the business would have to close. He had only three men at present, including an apprentice, and was absolutely without a bricklayer. Since war broke out he had released four men for service.—Have you any brothers? Yes, three.— Are any of them able to manage the business? No; they have not the slightest idea.--How long have you been in business? Thirteen years.— Conditional exemption was granted. Conditional Exemption for the Market Gardener.

A market gardener, who appealed on the grounds of national interests, stated that he was the tenant of a small farm, which com- prised 26 acres, 18 of which were arable. He was a married man, with five children, the eldest of whom was only eleven in September. Conditional exemption was granted.

Teamsman Gets Until October.

- - A farmer, who appealed for one of his teams- men, stated that it was impossible to manage unless he was allowed to keep his teamsmen. He had only two, and between them they had eight horses to look after.--Exemption until October Ist.

REVELRY AT STATHAM. "May Day" Scenes Draw A Large Holiday Crowd. • As was expected, the May Day festival at Statham last Monday drew quite a large crowd of visitors to the picturesque little hamlet. The day opened in a doubtful fashion, for heavy clouds hung threateningly over head, but 'by dinner-time they had disappeared, though the weather still remained anything but beautiful. The party assembled at Cross Brow and marched in procession round Booth's Hill to the Cross, headed by the Grappenhall Subscription Band, who played patriotic selections, then returned to the field via the Station. The principal attrac- tions on the programme were maypole and gar- land dancing, and the magnificent way in which these two old English dances were executed, brought forth great admiration from the large crowd of .spectators. Great credit is due to the children and their trainer, Miss E. Appleton. Considerable amusement was created when the wounded soldiers from Brookfield, who were the guests of the day, organised amongst them- selves a donkey race. The race was full of thrills and sensations, and many prospective winners came to grief, but the winning post was eventually reached by Private Jones, who re- ceived a great ovation from the crowd.

Catering arrangements were successfully carried out by Mrs. Thomas Appleton and .t staff of voluntary workers. ,

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC.

The clerk of the weather failed to smile at Statham last Monday.

Nevertheless, a very happy day was spent, and all wore their Sunday smile.

" Tommy " was quite a conspicuous figure in his grey suit. The "fairer sex" fouud him jolly good company.

Ice cream and donkey rides were included in the bill of fare.

Perhaps the most laughable scene of the day was " Tommy's ' donkey Derby."

" Tommy " mounted on a donkey is a sight not to be missed.

Who was the belle of the ball? That is the question I have been asked specially to answer.

Mr. Arthur Appleton and his sister were certainly the first pair to dance.

The new Chairman of the Council (Councillor Fred Lockyer) was the organiser of Monday'.; festival.

He deserves our congratulations for the splendid way in which everything was organised.

The funeral of the late Serg,4,nt Lewis took place at the Parish Church last Sunday.

Judging by the large crowd present, every home in Lymm must have been represented.

Amongst the large crowd congregated in the Churchyard could be noticed Sergeant Fiel3, our local D.C.M.

Though he wears his D.C.M. ribbon, he has not yet received his medal.

In the death of Mr. I. H. Evans, Lymm has lost one of its most popular men.

The venerable gentleman who served us with our medicine when we were children, will always live in our memory.

Tie took a prominent part in all local anfl Church affairs.

Aim son. Mr. F. E. Evans, is truly following In his father's footsteps.

Amongst the latest arrivals from the Front is Mr. A. B. Edwards, the well-known Y.M.C.A. worker.

All the Warrington "Press men" are to visit Lymm to-day—press(ing) engagement.

A little bit of a parody composed by Sergeant_ Major Madeley and sung by the local "Terriers."

THE RAGTIME ARMY.

We are the Ragtime Army, We are the "E. of C.," We cannot fight, we cannot shoot, What earthly use- are we? But when we get to Berlin The Kaiser he will say, "Och! Och ! Mien Gott! What a very fine lot, Are the 'Ragtime 'E. ef C."

We start work every morning Soon after five-thirty, And if we try to sleep till six We get three days' C.B. But our Sergeants won't allow us, They toss us out of bed, They shout, they yell, and then play H In the Ragtime "B. of C.'s."

And after, we've awakened, We get on Rouse Parade, In clean fatigue (without puttees), Chins must be freshly shaved; For if we're not too careful, It's on the peg we'll be, "Caps off," "Quick March," "Halt," and "Right Turn," And then it's sure C.B.

We're always bayonet fighting, Although we get no praise, Our Staff Instructor says to us: "A hymn of thanks we'll raise For the ever-useful Navy That's a-guarding of our shore. If it was not we'd get aishock; Class "shun,' now try once more."

We cannot rest on Sundays, - Huts are inspected then; Our blankets must be extra neat, And huts scrubbed out by ten. Our Colonel is a "corker," His sight is very keen, It's 'What? Oh, rot! Just peg this lot, It's the dirtiest hut I've seen."

Our S.-M. is a terror, In the ragtime "E. of C." Although he's smart and soldier-like, He's very hard to please; Defaulters dread his footstep, He's always after them; But all the same he plays the game With the chaps who try like men.

Here's best luck to the Army, Best luck to the "E. of C." We're the boys to work, we never shirk, Wherever we may be; From Chester, Lymm and Runcorn, You'll find them all come in; We're Cheshire cats, and the German rats Will suffer—in Berlin. Tune: "The Church's One Foundation."

LANCE-SERGT. HENRY BROOKES WOUNDED.

News was yesterday morning received in the village that Lance-Sergeant Henry Brookes, of the Cheshire Regiment, had been wounded. The news was conveyed through the medium of a letter sent by Sergeant-Major H. Clare, to the gallant sergeant's wife at Cheadle, and hence to his mother at Lymm. Sergeant Brookes, it will be remembered, was the first local soldier to receive a war decora- tion, when he was awarded the Medal of St. George (2nd Class) for heroic ambulance work in the field. He has just been home on .fur- lough, and returned to find that in his absenze he had been promoted from Corporal to Lance- Sergeant. He is a well-known local lad and is widely respected throughout the entire village. In civil life he was• emploYed as a garsener by Mr. Henry Champ, of Cheadle, 'late (if

Foxley House, Higher Lane, and worshipped at the Primitive Methodist Church. He took a keen interest in sport, and was looked upon by the football critics as a coming goalkeeper.

THE LATE SERGT. LEWIS.

Military Funeral And Memorial

Service.

Impressive scenes were witnessed at the Parish Church on Sunday afternoon, when the funeral (with semi-military honours) of the late Sergeant Lewis, whose death was reported in " The Examiner " last week, took place. Never in the history of Lymm has a larger crowd of ' residents congregated at a funeral; practically the whole of Lymm turned out to pay respect to the memory of one of their fallen heroes. The funeral procession, which started from the deceased's home at Rush Green and pro- ceeded to the church via Lymm Bridge, was headed by the guard and firing party, comprised of a detachment of men from the Third 4th Loyal North Lancashires, under the command of Captains Hooton and Richards, from the Lord Derby War Hospital at Winwick. Next came the coffin, covered with a Union Jack, placed on a wheeled bier, and drawn by several members of the local Volunteers, under the supervision of Sergeants S. D..Moston (Lymm) and T. Peters *(Oughtrington). Next followed the principal mourners and Mrs. Lewis (father and mother), Miss Lewis (sister), Mr. and Mrs. Tooley kbrother-in-law and sister), Mr. and Mrs. Brearley (brother-in-law and sister), Mr. and Mrs. Crossland (brother-in-law and sister), Mrs. Stratford and Miss Stratford
