"ALL'S WELL AT SEA ! "

Naval Hero At Lymm Describes The

North Sea Battle.

Gunner R. S. Hanableton, of the Marin 3 Artillery, a member of the crew of H.M.S. has this week been enjoying a well-earned rest at the residence of Mr. David Daniels, Booth's Hill.

Describing the great naval battle to an " Examiner " representative, the gathnt sailor said: "The Germans may shout for joy and bless Providence for sending them the thick fog that came over the scene o: operations early after the commencement of the battle. Only for that I am afraid that the German Navy would have been a thing of the past.

"It was about 40 aim. when the first round was fired, and for over 24 hours nine of our boats engaged 35 or 37 of theirs. If the weather conditions had only been favourable, we should have drawri them straight into the jaws oi Jellicoe's fleet. The Grand Fleet came to our rescue about 6.30 a.m., and remained in action for about two hours, but all the time the light was failing, and all that was visible was -he British line. For some considerable time we were firing at nothing but flashes. Attacked By Submarine.

"While we were returning to port after the battle we were attacked by a submarine, who fired at us three times, and`, of course, every time that she came on top we were quick to return the compliment. After the last shot we fired a cloud of blue smoke was seen to rise, and it is believed that the submarine was hit and sunk. Next time we have the privilege of meet- ing the rats of the. Kiel Canal' in the open we hope to do things proper." Gunner Hambleton has been in the Marines for four years, and has been on the -

for the past fourteen months. He is a single man, and hails from Heswall, Cheshire. He has a brother who has paid the supreme sacri- fice, and another who is at present serving in the Garrison Artillery. Prior to joining the Navy he was a member of the Cheshire Terri- torials. It is interesting to know that H.M.S. 's commander hails from Cheshire.

Death - of Well-known Wesleyan.—lt is with sincere regret that we record the death of Mrs. Waterworth, of Rothermay, Brook-road, Lymm, who passed away in the early hours of Saturday morning, June 10th. The deceased lady was the wife of Mr. John Waterworth, and with him she has been a member and an ardent worker in connection. with the Wesleyan Church here in many of its branches, always actuated by sterling devotion to its intents. Mrs. Water- worth's kindness as hostess to the ministers and local preachers of the circuit will, 1,6 a happy memory with all of them. As President of ale Ladies' Sewing Meeting she combined untiring energy with a gentle Christian spirit. Mrs. Waterworth was also a member of the British Women's Temperance Association and a member of the Free Church Council. Her life's aim is summed up in Tennyson's words: "To live pure, speak true, Follow the Christ the King." The interment took place at St. Paul's, Kersal, on Tuesday, when many beautiful floral tributes were sent by friends. The principal mourners were :—Mr. Water- worth (husband), Miss Waterworth and Miss G. Waterworth (daughters), Rev. H. Waterworth (brother-in-law), Mrs. Croughton (sister-in-law), Crumpsall; Mr. and Mrs. Tither (brother and sister-in-law), Higher Broughton; Messrs. R. E. and A. Tither (nephews), Higher Broughton; Misses B. and F. Tither (nieces). Higher Broughton; Mr. and Mrs. Adams, Cheethara Hill; Mr. and Mrs. McClallan, Dukinfield; Mrs. Bentley Devonport, Stockport; Mr. Ford, Alderley Edge; Mr. Whiteside. Alderley Edge; Mrs. Finch, Lymm; Mr. and Mrs. Bate, Lymm; and Miss Booth, Stretford. The Waterworths have three sons, and all of them are serving their King and country. Two of them were in Canada at the outbreak of war, and immediately joined the Canadian Forces. The other is at present stationed in Scotland.

The Missing Gun Licence.—At Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Ernest Wilkinson, Pool Plat Farm, Antrobus, was fined £1 for carrying a gun without a licence. When asked by P.C. Mellings whether he had a gun licence, defendant replied "Yes. It is in the house." Later he said he could not find it, but when it was shown the officer it was found to be issued the morning after the first visit of the officer. Defendant said he never used the gun for sporting purposes. He only had it to frighten the crows off His potato fields.

A LOSS TO LYMM.

Death Of Mr. Isaac Henry Evans.

In the death of Mr. I. H. Evans, of the Wood- lands, Higher-lane, Lymm has lost one of its grand' old men. For almost half a century this gentleman had supplied Lymm with its medi- cine, and many are the people who have blessed him for his acts of kindness in supplying free medicine, &c., in cases where 'he knew they were not in a position to pay. The late Mr. Evans was in his 87th year, and was born at Wigan. He served his apprenticeship as a chemist at Ormskirk, and later went to Southport to man- age a similar business. He came to Lymm somewhere about 1866, and since that time, up to his recent retirement, he served Lymm not only as a chemist, but in every way that a true citizen should.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
A Member of the Old Local Board.

Although a busy man, he always found time for local affairs, and for some considerable time was a member of the old Local Board, which met in Pepper-street. It was during the time that he was a member of this governing body that the great epidemic of smallpox broke out, and he, along with the other members, most of whom have passed away, had, many unpleasant duties to discharge in assisting the medical men to combat the disease. When this was at last done, Mr. Evans, along with others, was awarded special thanks for their co-operation and valued help. Ever since he came to Lymm he has been connected with the Parish Church,

===== 0002601_19160617_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
and for several years was one of the lay repre- sentatives at the Diocesan Conference.

A Prominent Mason.

The late Mr. Evans was a prominent Free- mason, and there was nothing about "Masonry" that he did not know. He was first a member of the "Earl" of Stamford" Lodge (1045), Altrineham, and he was the only surviving member of the "Earl of Chester" Lodge (1565) at Lymm. In this lodge he held several im- portant offices, which included those of charity representative and almener, and for 21 years held the office of secretary. He was elected Prov. G.S.B. in 1885 and also held the Provin- cial rank in the "Earl of Stamford" Lodge of Mark Masons, and the office of P.Z. in the "Elias Ashmole" Lodge, Warrington.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
The Funeral.

The funeral took place at the Parish Church at midday- on Tuesday, and was conducted by the Rector (the Rev. G. B. Thurston). During the service the hymn "0 God, our help in ages past," was sung, and Mr. W. B. Cooke, the church organist, played suitable funeral music, which included Chopin's Funeral March and "Oh, rest in the Lord" (Mendelssohn). The principal mourners were: Messrs. F. Evans (son), C. H. Wells (Warrington), W. B. Rich- mond (Chester), T. Ardern, H. S. Saville, E. Brown, W. E. Hillier, J. H. Critchley, Joseph Rogerson and D. Daniels. Brown, F. Druce, Wm. Allman, W. Boond and J. W. Gibbon. The Conservative Club and Association were repre- sented by Mr. T. F. W. Dixon and F. Garside. ===== 0002601_19160617_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
At the Graveside.

During the service the large crowd that had been unable to obtain admission to the church had gathered in the neighbourhood of the grave and the Volunteers and police met with con- siderable difficulty in. making a,passage for the Cortege to pass through. Everyone was anxious to see the last, for the circumstances of the gallant Sergeant's death had touched the hearts of all. As the Rector read the last burial rites sobs could be heard from every quarter of the crowd, and many a grey-haired veteran was observed to brush away a tear. Following the service the choir boys sang "Lord, now lettest thou Thy servant depart in peace," which was quickly taken up by the vocal members of the crowd, and the blending of the various voices, as they floated across the lake, sounded beauti- ful. Three sharp volleys fired over the dead hero's grave terminated the last earthly scene. Wreaths were sent by "Brother Dick and Alice," 'Mr. and Mrs. Stratford and family, Mrs. Torby (Warburton), • Mrs. Shaw and family, "The Neighbours," "His little friend, Jacky Warburton," and "Nieces and Nephews from Huddersfield."

The funeral arrangements were conducted by Messrs. G. A. Cooke, F. E. Evans and T. F. W. Dixon.

Joe, the coloured manservant, was wondering at the im- pudent urgency of the clamour that had startled him as he reached the bottom of the staircase. There he stood stock still to behold his young master, wild-eyed and dishevelled, rush from the library and seize him. "Don't open that door! I've killed a man, and they are after me." Still the sheriff hammered at the door, and, amid the din, the motor car agent from Richmond drove np in the new car, his mechanic driving a second car, in which they would return. "I am the sheriff," said the man to the new-corners. " I am after a man for murder and summon you to aid me." With a wave of the hand, Arthur jumped into the oar, and the machine bounded forward as a revolver bullet whizzed past the driver's head.

Due east ran the road until it turned sharply south, and ran parallel ivith the railway track. Horror of horrors! Thund4ring dawn the line was a Southern Pacific express.; Not 'a moment to lose. As he swung round the turning Arthur caught a glimpse of the other car. Surely the sheriff was gaining! Two miles further down the road crossed the railroad track. If he waited for the train to pass the sheriff would catch him. "Neck or noth- ing!" breathed Arthur, as he grimly resolved to race the train and beat it. Coaxing every ounce of power out of the engine, Arthur drove as he never drove before. The heart of the engine-driver almost ceased to beat as he realised the intention of the madman in the car. A. man must be in desperate need to endeavour to win the crossing before the heavy train. Nothing could he done, the train could not be checked in time. Now it was a ques- tion of seconds. Inch by inch the car, guided by a hand nerved to extreme need, gained. Side by side they raced to death. With eyes _glued upon the car, the engine-driver andY his mate saw Arthur turn sharply towards them. But the engine did not jolt. No, on the opposite side of the track the car was speeding to safety! And the sheriff? The driver of the second car gasped in terror as he pushed the brakes hard down, and waited till the train had passed. A cloud of dust a mile away marked the escape of the fugitive. But Arthur's troubles were not yet over. He failed to negotiate a turn in the road, and over a river bank dashed car and driver into the stream, fifty feet below.

Hagar was standing by her caravan at Blue Ridge, when the pigeon brought the message, " Come at once, mother, I need you." Like Rachel of old, Hagar mourned her children and would not be comforted. Now came a call from one of them. She drove at once to the house of mourning, and spared Esther further humiliation at the hands of the vindictive widow. Hagar took the girl to the camp.

ESPERANTO

As A Means Of Developing Good Relations.

The British Esperanto congress at York on Monday elected Mr. J. H. Warden (Edinburgh) as president. Dr. Andrew Scougal, chief in- spector of schools in Scotlaud, moved that the congress, believing that the use of Esperanto was one of the most rapid and efficient means of facilitating the development of good relations, commercial and otherwise, between Great Britain and Russia, should memorialise the ,RuSsian Ambassador in London and the British Ambassador in Petrograd on the subject, urging them to use their influence for the encourage- ment of the spread of the knowledge of the language. The motion was carried. On the proposition of Miss Taylor (Coventry) it was agreed ,to memorialise the. Prime Minister asking that in any new educational scheme likely to be introduced after the war more prominence should be given to Esperanto.

Ireland was first referred to as the Emerald Isle in a poem written in the year 1820 by Dr. Drennan.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 17, 1916.

RAILWAY MYSTERY!

Midnight Mail Held Up Near Warrington.

If, as G. R.. Sims says, "a mystery is, in a popular sense, that which cannot be easily ex- plained," then there was a mysterious happen- ing on the railway near Walton new junction on Wednesday midnight. The 8 o'clock mail train from Euston, which has swanked through Warrington at sixty miles an hour every night for years, Was coursing proudly along that splendid decline near Walton new junction when the engine.. man, fireman and guards were called to stop by the penetrating tinkle of the bell which rings in obedience to the pulling of the emer- gency chain running through the upper reaches of the carriages. The steam was switched off, the proud engine ceased to snort, and as the train eased up those in charge looked out anxiously to see whether, anyone should leap from a compartment and make for liberty across the fields of Arpley. But no one jumped. What could have happened? was the question worrying the train officials. Had there been ugly work—some glorious maiden menaced, some, card sharpers detected, .seldiers ,practising bayonet fighting and been too successful with, their thrusts, or had the Scottish travellers revelled in whisky so much in England that the nicht was full o' disturbance? All these ideas flooded the minds of officials, as they descended from their places with .their swinging lamps and ran along each side of the carriages shouting, "Hulleia! What's up?" Blinds went up with a rush and a click and dozens of nocturnal travellers showed their curiosity by leaning out of the windows, and their ignorance by asking questions. Unable to discover the cause as they walked outside and beginning to feel that there had been some horse-play, the guards stepped into the carriages, and, pacing the corridors, feraged. for . the culprit. They entered every compartment from one end of the train to the other, looking suspiciously at. those in first-class .as well as carriages, eyeing most scrupulously those who were sleeping or feigning sleep, and being almost mire one wen-bibulous laddie with three legs—one was a long whisky bottle— sprawled on the seat, was the origin of the trouble. But the officials were. only " ..tlmost sure"; they were not "quite certain," and they left him where he lay. The signal was given for the restart, the steam entered the cylinders of the restless engine, the wheels revolved, and, snorting, the proud Pegasus of the railroad resumed its prancing towards the poetic wilds of Scotland —via Wigan.

THE GREAT NEUTRAL.

Satire On The Way In Which We Are "Fed" With "News."

I am a Neutral Journalist who wanders round, Europe. lam absolutely impartial. lam abso- lutely trustworthy. My perfect integrity is vouched for at the head of all my articles. Pleasant it is to come over to London, sell one set of articles to the Boom Press and another to the Gloom Press, and then sit down with smil- ing face and begin an article foie Germany: "I sit in a hovel amongst the ruins of Fleet-street, with the wreck of the armoured fort of St. Paul's in view. I hear a stir outside. A wild mob of conscientious objectors is beating a re- cruiting officer to death. Such things happen hourly in defeated Albion." • My series of Lon- don,, Liverpool, Manchester and Birmingham— all in ashes—has proved so successful that I pro- pose to cover all the large towns and construct a Baedeker of ruins.

Yet I pride myself more on my work for England's Press. My German articles have all to be in the same vein. Only the Boom Press exists in Germany. But in England one can vary one's views and do artistic work. You must have read my story of the struggle for the last sausage in a Frankfort butcher's shop— how the troops intervened and the crowd at- tacked them, and how 'ultimately 1,400 civilians were mown down with machine guns—and the sausage was eaten by the General 'Officer- corn- mending the Army Corps that suppressed the rising. You must also hnve seen my description of the Kaiser—his white hair, bent shoulders, deathlike look as he passed, protected by his Guards from the wild fury of the Berlin mob. Of course I have another Kaiser, the bright smiling man whose youth seems to have been renewed by the war, who waves his hand to the madly enthusiastic crowds waiting round the Palace for a glimpse of their divinity. You must have read my secret interviews with distinguished Germans, who whispered to me that Hindenburg had thrown down his sword and declared that if the useless slaughter did not cease he would march on Berlin. I have told you their promises of bloody revolu- tions and fierce risings. Also I have given you. interviews with other distinguished Germans, who confided to me that now Germany could turn out one submarine and one Zeppelin every week-day, and two on Sundays, and I have thrilled you with the details of the great trade war which will come directly peace is declared, when Germany will win back all her wealth by selling everything fifty per cent, below cost. How my dinners vary in that strange Teutonic land! I pay twenty marks for two tiny slices of fish, a thin piece of indigestible potato bread, and a section of rancid sausage. At other times I spend two marks and get a delightful meal which could not be procured in a London restaurant for five shillings. I walk through Berlin and see scarcely a cripple or a wounded man. I let you know that ninety-five per cent. of German wounded, owing to the skill of German doctors. go back to the Front in a week. To other English readers I confide that all the maimed, wounded and blind are sent into the very centre of Germany. There are huge did- tricts without a whole man in them. Did you ,ask for the actual facts? I will give you one—and it is this: the only persons in Germany whose waist-measurements have in- creased in the war are the neutral journalists.-- "Punch."

===== 0002601_19160617_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
DECLINING BIRTH-RATE.

State Bonuses For Children And Improved Housing. Many suggestions for arresting Great Britain's declining birth-rate arc made by the Commission of Inquiry which was appointed in 1913 by the National Council of Public Morals. The result of the Commission's labours, in part, is now presented in a volume published by Messrs. Chapman and Hall, Ltd. The evi- dence was too voluminous to be published in its entirety, but nothing of importance has been deleted by the editor. It may be added that the expenses of the Commission and the publi- cation of the report have been wholly defrayed by the National Council. The report is divided into five chapters, and there is also an "Addi- tion to the Report" signed by all the members of the Commission except the Chairman, which makes further recommendations Among other things, it declares that:— Without any desire for Imperial domina- tion or commercial exploitation, or military subjugation of other races Britain must, in view of what has been advanced, regard with gravest concern her falling birth-rate, and take such practical steps as may be within her power to arrest the decline, and, if pos- sible, restore the rate to a higher figure.' Some Recommendations. Some of the suggestions are as follows The' living" wage. State bonuses to families, when the earn- ings do not amount to .2100 or £l2O a year; for all children who attain the age of 14 years. Further remission of income tax for each child.. Facilities for a good, cheap education aboye the standard of the elementary school. Improvement in housing accommodation. Attention is also drawn to the appalling ,infantile and child mortality, " which must be `ascribed to preventable causes." "We feel that the care of the mother before child-birth is as important as the care of the child after its birth." The decline is not, to any important extent, it is added, due to alterations in the marriage rate or to a rise of the mean age at marriage, but is due to conscious limitation. The decline is more marked among the more prosperous classes. The Commission puts on record its opinion that the housing question, both in town and country, makes the rearing of large families by the working classes a matter of great difficulty, and also affects the birth-rate.

IN THE COURTS.

FINE OF £lO AND COSTS.

Van Dweller's Fraud On Small

Shopkeeper.

A fine of .£lO and 15s. costs was imposed •by the Borough magistrates on Tuesday, on Jane Davies, or Marshall, a van.dweller, on a charge of obtaining by false pretences 17s. 6d. in cash and articles valued 188., the property of Mary Jane Clay, 164, Winwick-road. Prisoner pleaded guilty.

The Chief Constablf said the prosecutrix, whose husband was on the sick list, was struggling for her existence, and kept the shop in order to eke out a living. Along with a small boy, prisoner visited the shop shortly after twelve 'o'clock on June 6th, and declared that she could brink 14 families or 35 wc:rkpeople to deal with prosecutrix for the supply of pro- visions, &c., and at the same time added that she had been recommended to Mrs. Clay for good'home-made bread. Mrs. Clay told her that IShe did not sell home-made bread, but had other 'bread equally as good. Eventually, the prisoner Ilwas supplied with goods amounting to 10s., and she stated that in view of her recommendations, the prosecutrix might compensate her by doing some business with her. Accordingly. Mrs. Clay purchased a rug from the prisoner for 17s: 64., which was later found to be only worth be- tween ss. and 7s. 64. Prisoner then left, but the prosecutrix, becoming suspicious, informed the police, with the result that prisoner was arrested at Prodsham by Detective-Sergeant Summers. After the arrest, the husband of the prosecutrix received a visit from a man, who offered to pay 17s. 6d. for the rug; for which, he said, he had beenssent.. He also offered to pay the money due to the nrosecutrix for the goods she had parted with. This man, added the Chief Constable, had not been sent by anybody-so far as the prosecution was concerned, neither had the prisoner -sent him. , Mrs. Clay parted with the goods and purchased the rug because she thought the prisoner's statement regarding intro- ducing customers was true. Camped Near Jockey Bridge.

Mr. T. S. Steel, who appeared for the prisoner, said the latter's statement was true to a certain extent. She did belong to a large family which travelled about together, and had camped near Jockey Bridge until they had been prohibited from doing so. Prisoner had in- formed him that when she sold the rug to Mrs. Clay, ,b Auld the latter that if she was not satisfied she could have her money back again. Mr. Steel appealed to the Bench not to send prisoner to gaol. She was a young woman with one child, and had nothing previously against her.

The Chief Constable thought it was a very bad case. In fining her the above-mentioned amount, the Chairman of the Bench (Mr. J. Rymer Young) said it was a case of deliberate and heartless fraud.

The man who interviewed the husband of the prosecutrix was cautioned, the Bench telling him that he had narrowly escaped being sent t;) prison:

TWO SHILLINGS ON ACCOUNT.

A Liverpool labourer named Andrew Watson, who gave an address of 37, Buckingham-street, Liverpool, was; remanded at the Warrington Police Court on Saturday on a charge of obtain- ing 2s. by means of a trick from Mrs. Mary Wood, milliner. 76, Winwick-street. , Prisoner went to the shop of the prosecutrix and arranged to repair a window blind for 3s. 6d. Before commencing the work he bor- rowed 2s. on account, and whilst .Mrs. Wood was engaged with a customer he' left with the job unfinished. Information was given to the police, and prisoner was arrested on the Central Station, where he had taken a ticket for Liverpool.

Prisoner declared he was only called away by a friend to have a drink with him.

Inquiries are to be made with regard to his previous character.

FAMED TO REPORT.

When notified by the military authorities to report himself for the Army, Thomas Allen, an Irish farm. labourer, of no settled address, absented himself and went back to Ireland. On his return a few days ago he was arrested for failing to report under the Military Service Act. At the Warrington Occasional Gourt on Mendav he was fined 40s. and handed over to the military authorities. Accused was formerly employed as a farm hand at Rixton.

TWO MEN NAMED BURROWS-AND A CHEQUE.

On a charge of stealing a cheque for £1 Bs. from an Army Paymaster, John Burrows, labourer, 10, Hill-street, was remanded tor seven days by the magistrates at the Warring- ton Police Court on Saturday. It appears that an old soldier named John Burrows, whose home address was Hemsby, Norfolk, had been lodging at 10, Hill-street, but owing to ill-health was removed to the Warring- ton Union Infirmary. Later the prisoner, whose name by a strange coincidence was John Burrows, went to reside at the same house in Hill-street, and while there a cheque for £1 Bs. addressed to John Burrows was delivered from an Army Paymaster intended for the man who had gone into the hospital. Prisoner received the cheque and cashed it. Prisoner had enlisted but was discharged as being medically unfit. In order to complete inquiries a remand was necessary.

Prisoner said he was innocent of any attempt to defraud.

INSPECTOR OF "SPECIALS" ASSAULTED. A burly .Yorkshireman, named William Daw- son, of no settled abode, was fined 19s. 6d.—or, in default, 14 days' imprisonment—for being drunk and disorderly and assaulting Special Inspector Mason.

Mr. Mason said that, in company with Special Acting-Sergeant Dickens, he saw the prisoner in a very drunken, condition, singing outside the Hinpodrome. They cdutioned him and re- quested him to go home, but he .refused and assumed a- threatening 'attitude and tried to assault witness with a .heavy stick. The pri- soner was so violent that the assistance of two civilians had to be requisitioned to help to get him to the Police Station. On the way the prisoner repeatedly kicked witness in the body and legs.

EMPLOYMENT IN MAY.

Increased Wages For 370,000 Workers.

The Board of Trade -"Labour Gazette," re- porting on State employment in May, says it continued at a very high level in all industries directly affected by the requirements of the Forces, and in other industries good as a whole. Calls to the Colours accentuated the shortage qf labour, and women- were required to.dbe sub- 'stituted to a .much greater -degree. Three hun- dred and seventy thousand workpeople benefited by wage increases, which reached a total of .R 31,000 per week. The total number of disputes beginning in May was 41, the workpeople involved number- ing 30,439, compared with considerably over 54,000 in the previous month. The estimated total aggregate duration of all disputes during the month was 307,400 working days, compared with 654,600 in the previous month.

-Unfortunately it Often happens that the man who is most fond of his • home never succeeds anywhere else.

Ate Too Much.

In tirleit-e-r—to I.oid Murray, Sidney Smith says :—" You are, I hear, attending more to diet than heretofore. If you wish anything like happiness, in the fifth act of life, you should pay more attention to the amount you eat and drink. Did I ever tell you my calculation about eating and drinking? Having ascertained the weight of what I did live upon, I found that between ten and seventy years of age I had eaten and drunk 44 one-horse waggon-loads of meat and drink more than would have pre- served me in life and health. The value of this mass of nourishment is considered to be worth £7,000 sterling. It occurred to me that I must, by voracity, have starved to death more than one hundred. This is a frightful calculation, but irresistibly true—and I think, dear Murray, your waggons would need additional hones." •

During the .past week they had had the pleasure of distribut- ing over 300 war workers' badges to members en- gaged in making comforts for the soldiers. On Friday they had a request from-Sir Edward Ward for comforts, which were to be sent to the soldiers in Mesopotamia.

Consequently the cheque would be sent on to her by post. Mrs. Rylands has since written to Mr. Patrick, saying she had been on holiday and had only found the letter upon her return. Mr. Patrick, however, had had courage enough to go forward and ask for the prizes, and to him they were very thankful. (Applause)

Infectious Disease.

The new cases of infectious diseases reported in Warrington last week were :—Scarlet fever, 3; tuberculous phthisis, 2; measles, 7. There remained in the hospital 34 scarlet fever patients, one enteric fever, three diphtheria, one "other disease," and 19 phthisis case.

Are Our Homes Worth Defending.? Speaking at a meeting held on Cornhill, Bewsey-street, under the Citizens' Temperance League; last night, the Rev. T. T. James asked whether our homes were worth defending and whether they were worth the lives that were being given in France and on the seas in protecting them? Homes which were not redolent of purity, sobriety, and honesty, he said, were not deserving of the defence of the brave lads who were dying for England. The reverend gentleman made an earnest and eloquent appeal to his hearers to-be temperate, contending that this was one of the best ways to improve ourselves and our homes; in fact, tem- perance was essential to the leading of a decent I life.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. J. Griffiths presided over the meeting.

E TRIBUNALS.

FA LITARY OBJECTION

To Taxi Proprietor's Condi- tional Exemption.

One of the most interesting cases heard by the Warrington Tribunal came under notice at yesterday's sitting, before the Deputy-Mayoi (Councillor Dr. Joseph) presiding, Aldernlan Tinnion, Messrs. J. J. Whitley and J. Elwell. It was where the military authorities, through their representative, Ciptain Mathams, having taken objection to the granting at a previous hearing of conditional exemption to a local taxi cab and motor proprietor, the appeal came up for further consideration. Mr. T. S. Steel represented him. It was 'explained by Captain Mathams that the Tribunal granted conditional exemption to the appellant in March last on the ground that the occupation was included in the reserved lists. They now claimed that the applicant did not come within the certified trades.

Mr. Steel pointed out that the exemption was not granted on-those grounds, but on a genet. Al business claim, and it was now a question for the Tribunal to decide whether the circum- stances had altered.

Last week, Captain Mathams continued, he applicant went to Liverpool and appealed at the County Tribunal in respect of one of his employes—a motor mechanic, who, he stated, was the only man he had in his employ with mechanical knowledge. When the case was beard in March the proprietor stated that out of his twelve drivers, eight had enlisted. It was further urged by the applicant that he was engaged in carrying excess mails for the Post Office, and also carted goods for local munition works. The applicant led the Tribunal to bp- lieve that, as far as the business was concerned, he was the important individual. When appeal- ing for his mechanic at Liverpool he urged that the mechanic was doing exactly the same work as himself.

Applicant: No, I didn't say that. I said he was the only man I had with a knowledge of the mechanism of a motor.

Captain Mathams: Then you have no mechani- cal knowledge. Applicant: I am not a fully qualified motor mechanic.

Captain Mathams: The onlY thing then you can do is drive?

Applicant: Yes, and generally keep an eye on the business.

Captain Mathams : Have ,any of your other men mechanical knowledge P—No.

Mr. Steel contended that the exemption was granted on general business grounds. The appellant was a married man and had charge of the business, with a capital of £2,000 in- volved. Ile was the sole proprietor of two garages. At the outbreak of war his brother, with whom he was in partnership, enlisted, and the appellant was keeping the business going for him as well as himself. The circumstances had not altered except that two more of his drivers had enlisted, leaving him with only one male and one female driver.

The Chairman: The only question is as to the contradictory statement, in which he made out that he was indispensable, whereas it turns out that instead of him being indispensable, his man was also!

The decision was adjourned. Master Electrician's Application.

The adjourned appeal of a single man in business as an electrician again came up for consideration. The case was adjourned a few weeks ago in view of a statement made by the applicant that he had applied to the Ministry of Munitions for badges for himself and his employes.

He now stated that the Ministry of Munitions would not grant badges unless a firm were en- gaged on direct Government contracts. He had contracts for several firms in Warrington directly engaged on munitions.

Aide-man: tinnion : Why didn't you get them to apply for a badge for you and your workmen? It can be done.

, , Applicant said his manager had been granted a certificate-by the recruiting officer, as he was engaged in a reserved trade.

The- Chairman: It see-ni-S-- strange that the manager of the business can get a certificate and the owner cannot get one, Mr. Steel (who represented the appellant) : We have to come here and apply for ene. The Chairman: Assuming you went into the Army, what would happen to your business? Applicant: It would go. I would not feel dis- posed to go and leave the business in charge of my manager.

Mr. Steel pointed out that since the adjourn- ment four more employes had gone into the Army.

In this case also the decision was postponed. Marine Store Dealer's Complicated Case.

The appeal of a local marine store dealer in business in Allen-street, who was represented by Mr. Steel, Occupied some considerable time. 'the claim was adjourned a few weeks ago to ascer- tain the result of the medical examination of another brother who was in Ireland. The report of the doctor nominated by the military authorities in Ireland was submitted. and the Tribunal granted exemption on medical grounds in his case.

Mr. Steel submitted that tlo applicant had full charge of the business. They had never contended at the previous h.-mring that the other brother, who was exempted on medical grounds. was of much assistance in the. carrying on of the business.

The Clerk (Mr. A. T. H.allaway): Axe the Tribunal justified in conlbulcng that in view of the decided fall in the price of hair the claim for the national importance of the business is not as great as it .was previousli? Applicant: We have contracts with several tanners, and the fall in price does not 'mean that our supplies are not wanted by our customers.

In further cross-examination, applicant admitted that a third brother who was formerly in. the business left Warrington about Septem- ber to set up business on his own account in Liverpool.

By Captain Mathams : Is there any reason why you cannot co-operate with him in your busi- ness ?—There are private reasons. By Mr. Hallaway : If your brother has a busi- ness in Liverpool and resides there can you explain why he attested in Warrington in March and gave as his address 55, Allen-street? —He was travelling to and from Liverpool for the first few months. Whatever mistake he made I am not responsible. Are not the business premises in Liverpool tenanted by ydur eldest brother?—No. It is divided between the two of them:

Exemption until the end of July was granted. The Chairmen said that in arriving at a decision the Tribunal were influenced by the fact that two exemptions had been granted to mem- bers of the family on medical grounds, and that the brother who went to Liverpool and who was up to recent date the general manager, left somewhat suddenly. The decision in the case of the applicant was final. .

===== 0002601_19160617_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
Alderman Tinnion and the Dairyman.

Mr. T. S. Steel appeared on behalf of a cow- keeper and dairyman, who applied for the exemption of his son, aged 22, who assisted him with the business. It was stated that the young man had been engaged in the business since he left school. The stock comprised 24 milking cows.

Alderman Tinnion: Do they never see any grass?--We buy green food for them. They are never turned out into fields.

The Chairman (Dr. Joseph): Is there not a new mechanical apparatus for milking cows Yes, but I don't think it has proved'successful. In reply to Alderman Tinnion, witness stated that he had two other sons at school. Cannot they milk ?—No. • Alderman Tinnion: I could milk at that age, although I could not' do it now!

Witness, continuing, said women could not do the work as it wa's too heavy. His son had to clean out ,the shippons, load and unload the provender as it arrived.

Captain Mathams: Do the cows go blind? Witness: No! There is plenty of light in the shippons.

in reply to Mr. Steel, he added that.there were over 7,000 cows housed similarly in Liver- pool.'

Exemption until August 31st was granted. What We Have And The Sccts Haven't.

Window cleaners are, according to Captain Mathams, unknown in Scotland (no libel on Scottish cleanliness 0. The remark was elicited when an application was made by Mr. T. Proud on behalf of the Warrington District Window

Cleaning Co., Ltd., for their foreman window cleaner, a married man with two children. If the applicant were taken the whole of the original staff would have gone. They had three men who had been rejected. They had tried women cleaners, but they were unsuitable for ladder work. They were only fit to do the ground work. Captain Mathams : Can the windows not be cleaned from the inside? Mr. Proud: No. We are not doing much house work. Our work is for Government and public bodies and a large amount of shop work. En addition they had the contract for the ele- mentary schools in the borough. Captain Mathams : If you go to Scotland there is no such thing as a professional window cleaner. _

Exemption until the end of August was granted. On the application of Mr. W. B. Forshaw, solicitor, conditional exemption was granted to Mr. William Walter Winstanley, a partner in a firm of painters and decorators, who, since March, has been attached to the French Red Cross Society as an ambulance driver. - A hairdresser, 23 years of age, and single, applied for exemption on the ground that he was the sole support of a mother and sister, who were dependent on the business. The applicant will be notified. of the Tri- bunal's decision at a later date. A Patriotic Family.

A young man, single, claiming exemption on domestic grounds, stated that his father, who was a pensioner, re-enlisted at the outbreak of war and had died on duty with the home Army. The allowance would cease at the end of June. Three other brothers were serving; two in India and one in England. Two sisters were working and one was engaged at home, whilst two younger brothers were still at school. Conditional_ exemption was granted..

• On the application of Mr. W. Hodgkinson, of the firm of Messrs. Hodgkinson, Ltd., Bridge. street, conditional exemption was granted to three members of his staff, namely, furnishing department manager, upholsterer, and the buyer for the dress material and ladies' mantles department. Three months' exemption was granted to the cutter in the gentlemen's tailoring department. An optimistic coal dealer, single, aged 31, who was granted exemption until the end of August on business grounds, expressed the opinion that 'he thought the war will be over by then. Applicant was thanked by one of the Tri- bunal for his information. In addition to selling coal, applicant was a pig breeder, but he had sold his last lot last week-end. His father was a contractor in business on his own account, but it was impossible for him to carry on the two businesses if applicant had to go. Mr. J. W. Kay, grocer and provision dealer, Battersby-lane, applied for the exemption of his shop manager, aged 27, and single. The only other employe was aged 70. He had taken every step to obtain another man, but had only received one inquiry, and When it was known there was work to do the man would not take the position.. Two of his men left to go into munition works.. He had only this young man left to carry on the business. They had a young lady at another branch shop, but she had given in her notice. • Temporary exemption until the end of Sep- tember was granted.

===== 0002601_19160617_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR THE LAND.

Master-Farmers And Some Sons

Exempted.

A number of master farmers and some farmers' sons were conditionally exempted by the Warrington Rural District Tribunal at their sitting on Wednesday afternoon. Before commencing the hearing of cases, the Chairman (Mr. George Mather) expressed the hope that those who got any kind of postpone- ment or exemption would join. the Volunteer Training Corps, Mr. T. S. Steel appeared on behalf of a Hol,. linfare farmer, who asked for the exemption of his son-in-law, who worked as a teamsman. There were sixty-six acres and only the two of them to look after them. His youngest son had just joined the Army. Applicant had been unable to obtain women owing to the proximity of his farm to a steel works. Although he was nearly seventy years old, and his wife was aged 64 years, they had both to do their share of work.

Conditional exemption. A farmer with three holdings at Fearnhead, covering in all 105 acres, made an. application. He had six cows and four horses, and was un- able to do anything himself, owing to being 72 years old. He wanted the exemption of a son aged 26 years. Another son aged 35 years had been exempted. He had two other sons, one aged 33 and the other 14. The difficulty was that he had three different holdings. A Member of the Tribunal: It is rather a mistake for .a man to hold such a number of farms.

Another Member: You are on very debatable ground there.

The Military representative (to applicant): Are any of your sons in the Army?—Ao. Exemption until the 30th September. School Children as Farm Hands.

Mr. Steel also appeared for a Woolston farmer, who asked for the exemption of a son, as well as a man. He farmed 49 acres mostly' of market garden produce. Previous to the outbreak of the war he had seven men; now he only had three men and a boy. He 'had to employ school children at night in order to get through the work. Every bit of land he had was cultivated. Last year he put 865 tons of manure into the ground. There were two sons on the farm, one being married and the other single. Cases adjourned for a month.

A Rixton farmer wanted a son aged 23 years exempted. Besides this farm he has one in Lincolnshire, another son being in charge of it, and he (the applicant) visits there twice a week to see that everything is going on all right. At home applicant also had three more sous, aged 19 (a cripple), 17, and 15 years. Application refused, whereupon the applicant said it was impossible for him to manage the two farms. He pleaded hard, and was given an exemption of fourteen days. Owner of Three Farms.

The owner of three farms at Culeheth and Rixton had no other labour but that given by his four sons. Exemption had already been granted to one, two had been rejected, and he was now applying for the other helper on the farm. He had a fifth son, who did not work at home, and had joined the Navy, but had not yet been called up.

Appeal dismissed, whereupon the applicant (an old man) said: Well, you can take me as well, then.

Conditional exemption was granted to a Woolston farmer's son, aged 40 years 4 The farmer has a son already with the Colours, and he had only this son to help him. The applicant himself was suffering from a complaint which might be fatal if he did hard work. Asked if he had any women on the farm, the applicant replied, "I have only two women, and bad 'uns they are. " Sad Domestic Situation,

A sad domestic situation was revealed by an applicant from Fearnhead. Employed by a Warrington firm of grocers, he himself, he said, suffered from rheumatism, lumbago, and 'bad -feet; his wife had a serious complaint; and his three children were so poorly that they could not attend school. Two of them he hop4d to get into a sanatorium very soon. Regarding himself, he had been passed "oy a Medical Board as being fit for general service, but he denied that he was so, and handed in a letter from his employers, which stated that during last year he wasunable to attend work for as many as ten weeks. .

The Military Representative (Mr. Furness) pointed out that the Medical. Board had signi- fied their approval of him for the Army, and, therefore, 1--;<, health did not concern that Tribunal. They could only deal with the con- dition of his family. • The applicant aPpealed to the Tribunal to view the whole of the circumstances in a human. spirit. He did not want to evade military ser- vice himself, but if he had to go his wife would have. that burden .on her shoulders besides her own illness, and the prospect of her children being taken away from her. A member of the ,Tribunal remarked that he had been very unfortunate with his family. Adjourned until the end of September.

Prepared For Bad Weather.

The following is told by a young bride-elect who was ordering her trousseau in England before going out ta New Zealand to be married. The dressmaker suggested only very warm clothes, and when the young lady demurred at this, asserting that the climate of New Zea- land is a beautifully mild one, she was imme- diately informed: " I assure you, madam, you are mistaken, for that, of course, is where the frozen meat comes from."

===== 0002601_19160617_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
STAGE AND KINEMA.

Royal Court.

"I suppose the first question an interviewer puts to you is about your big, blundering boots," I said to Mr. Fred Kitchen, the eminent comedian, whose revue, "All Eyes," has been drawing large audiences this week. ." Yes," he replied, "and I always tell them to ask Charlie Chaplin about them."

We both laughed, and he developed the idea by contending that Cinematograph Charles has copied the modes of dress Mr. Kitchen has employed for over twenty years, particularly in the large, awkward feet, the small " dickie," hat, and the light, elastic cane. His manner- isms also.

Mr. Kitchen enjoyed eleven years' glory qs principal comedian to Fred Karno, and it was during the sojourn with Fred's company that Mr. Chaplin studied him. Charlie, we know, is a 'cute fellow.

Born within a stnne's throw of a theatre, and the son of a noted Clown, Mr. Kitchen, has been associated with the stage almost as long as fate could allow. He was forty-two years old on Thursday, and a telescopic glance in the remoter reaches of his career reveals that he made his first stage appearance when six weeks old. He—in those days, like all children, he was known as "it "—was the realistic touch in a christening ceremony in a play, but cannot recollect the name of it. At such an early age the strength of his memory was like the water they sprinkled on his beatific brow—weak!

He has known no other sphere of earning a living since. The stage was his first love and he has found complete satisfaction. It has given him fame. He was not very old before the laughs of friends and others irresistibly com- pelled him to believe he was a funny man, and I his innate gift has carried him through life in comparative opulence. Almost all countries on the Continent and our colonies have welcomed him with the enthusiasm we. know' of in Eng- land, including Warrington.

"All Eyes" has only come to us at the end of its tour with Mr. Kitchen, for next week but one he starts rehearsing at the London Opera House in Mr. Oswald Stoll's revue, "Look Who's Here!" He will be one of a constella- tion of "stars," for Billy Merson, the Ritchie Troupe, Miss Ethel Levey and Miss Florence Smitheson also are to shine brightly in the cast.

Kitchen's diminutive hand-mate, Gulliver (Mr. Willie Atom) has also been a great object of interest. They have been together for about twelve months—soon after "All Eyes" opened in the Provinces—and the little fellow is within a few months of his big leader as far as age goes. It is on the cards that Willie may partner Fred in "Look Who's Here !"

Miss Ella Ward, the fair lady, who has been acting as principal barmaid at most perform- ances, is the wife of Mr. Kitehen. Her dress cost just under twenty guineas.

We are to have another of Mr. Lodge Percy and Miss Schrier's plays next week in "When the Angelus is Ringing." These writers are skilful in discerning the public taste, and we may be sure of ail:exciting and eventful time. Hippodrome.

lienading a programme of general excellence and variety are George Willie, West and McGinty, as Jerry and Co., builders. They have been entertainers all their lives, and by studying builders from the outside have got an amusing knowledge of the inside of the trade. Their make-up as navvies and a carpenter is true in every detail, the scenery characterises sites in process of defacement by the erection of jerry houses, and the mannerisms burlesqhe entertainingly the vagaries of the British workman.

To develop their production to perfection these gentlemen—for they are such off the stage; it is clothes that make the navvies—have studied carefully actual building operations. Everywhere they go they aim at introducing local idiosyncracies. They are just a little too late to watch Warrington Bridge grow.

When in France they discovered that their show was not going as strongly as they ,wished, so they sought for the reason, found it. rectified their errors, and achieved fame. They dis- covered that the French wear overalls, the trousers being tied with string round the ankles, that the shafts of the spades are longer so that the workmen will not cringe with backache, and that they do not bustle at their work

About six months after the Empress of Ireland had an untimely end and Mr. H. B. Irving lost his famous brother, the great Shakespearean actor was performing at the same theatre in Glasgow as Jerry and Co.

Though a considerable time had elapsed Mr. Irving had not recovered from the blow received by his brother's death, and it was only after great persuasion that he consented to witness Jerry and Co. go through their act. Mr. Irving forgot his great sorrow and laughed as heartily as anyone. But the point of the story is that afterwards Mr. Irving visited the stage, went up to a " super " and congratulated him most enthusiastically on the show. He thought the " super " was the leader!

Mr. Frank IT. Fox's character studies are splendidly convincing, notably those of "The Punch and Judy Man," "The Morning After " and "The Tinker." This last is not after Bunyan. A remarkable feature is the rapidity with which the changing characters are staged by Mr. Fox. He has wonderful versatility and capacity for representation.

The music and words of his songs originnEed in Mr. Fox himself, and these are of such a popularity that he has sung several into the phonographs of the Pathe firm.

An excellently varied programme is announced for next week, the items including: The Crazy Sailors, in "Fun Aboard H.M.S. Lively"; Jep- son and Soprano, in a delightful vocal musical act; The Decars and Tomato; and Walter Aubrey.

The Kinemas. "Bella Donna" is fully justifying the boom given it during the past few weeks in advertise- ments. It is a wonderful picture. During the first part of next week we are to have Miss Marguerite Clark in "Helene of the North," and in the second half Miss Fanny Ward in "The Marriage of Kitty."

Miss Ward is the celebrated 4.lct.Fess" who married a millionaire, was divorced, and has now added to her adventures by appearing as cinema "star." She received £2,000 for her appearance in this one play.

London theatregoers will remember Miss Ward's, sensational appearance in London in March, 1908, when, in one of the scenes of "The Marriage of William Ashe," she walked on to the stage in a costume which was one .laze of diamonds, valued at over £200,000.

At the Picture house patrons have been de- lighted with "A Queen's Love" during the first part of the iveek, and the " star " picture now appearing is entitled "Woman's Weak- ness." it is full of dramatic situations.

Next week the principal pictures during the first and second portions respectively will be "Stronger Than Death "- and' "A Tragic Circle." Advance agents speak highly of them.

The second episode of "The Diamond From the Sky" has been shown this week at the Pavilion, Sankey Green, and The Grand, Bridge Foot. Its startling qualities are well known to " Examiner " readers. Next week the third in- stalment will be shown, in au—ultion to a splen- did selection of other films.

At the Pavilion the two big films for the first part are entitled "The Silent Witness" and "Rupert of Hentzau," and for the second part "Lola."

At the Grand, the famous novel, " The White Hope," will be the principal picture in the first half, and in the second the beautiful story " Infelice." These 'Subjects speak for them- selves. FOYER.

PA D GATE.

Walking Day.-1 meeting was held in the Church House on Wednesday evening, repre- sentatives being present from the Wesleyan and Church of England Sunday Schools. It Air° decided to have a short walk of the Sundal school children on Saturday, July Ist, each school going to its respective church for tee, &c. After tea the usual adjournment will be made to the field, but no roundabouts, swings, Ste., are to be allowed this year.•

Boy Scouts.—On Saturday the boys of the 2nd Padgate Troop of Scouts indulged in a route march and also • dispatch carrying. The rendezvous of the dispatch carriers was Win" wick Church. After allowing the main boa ten minutes start, the carriers started. It vilif a very sharp run and all were glad of the res' of a few minutes' duration that ensued at the, finish of the run. The march home via Orfora concluded a good afternoon's work.

GOSSIP* A soldier at the Front, writing to a friend of mine, says the roads near the firing line dre worse even than Manchester-road. That road must have made an impression on the mind of our warrior when remembrance suggests remarks like this amid shell-made • craters and bomb-swept thoroughfares But he has been away for some time and has not, per- chance, been along Manchester-road since it was tar-sprayed? There was a modest feast in the Council. Chamber on Saturday, when the Mayor enter- tained the Working Men's Infirmary Committee to afternoon tea. As is usual when his Worship is the presiding genius, there wa,s not only,enough but to spare. He did not go out into the highways and bye- ways to find those who would be well qualified to eat up the surplus stock; but he sent to the Old Men's Parliament in the Park and bade its members come to the banqueting hall. Parliament quickly adjourned and was quickly installed in the Town Hall, where his Worship and Mr. Mitchell waited upon them, paying suitable respect to the fact that the father of the House was eighty years of age and the baby of the Assembly was sixty-four. , They were regaled on the best that the con- fectioners' art can conceive, the finest tea that the Town Hall kitchen can produce, and `f something " in a decanter that- the secret recesses of the Mayor's Parlolir opportunely disgorged. The Mayor said that any of the men who had a cold might have a little of this something in their tea. The whole Parliament—except the two babies, I think they were—had a cold. At the end his Worship added a cigar qnrl a buttonhole and Parliament was prorogued after one of its most agreeable and productiv, sessions. I thought no one could silence the Rev. A, M. Mitchell, but I see that the printer of his Parish Magazine—the "Excelsior "—has inti- mated that in future his contributions must not exceed four pages, owing to the shortage of paper. . The reverend gentleman has been in the habit of writing as many as eight or ten pages, and will, therefore, have to curtail his output con- siderably. This should be quite easy. A Parish Magazine is not the place to talk about Woman Suffrafri jig-gotarianism, and othei secular subjects, agillartestingly as Mr. Mitchea writes about them. I notice that the cover of his Magazine still contains the announcement—" All seats free; no pew monopoly." Mr. George E. Edwards, Bruche View, staff electrician at Messrs. Rylands Brothers, was electrician on the Hampshire for the first two years she was in commission. She was a bonny ship, he says; and he was especially grieved that she should have perished in such tragic circumstances. Rotherham was Mentioned at the `Board of Guardians on Wednesday, and Mr: W. J. Shaw, remarked inquiringly, "Isn't that in York- shire?" Mr. George Mather pounced upon him with, "No, it's in Asia Minor!" He was quite Shavian in his smartness. The Government have decided on the pur- chase, through the Liquor Traffic Control Board, of the whole of the breweries and public- houses—the latter some 300 in number—in the city of Carlisle and its immediate neighbour- hood. I wonder whether the Board will cast their eyes Warrington way.

be held at St. Barnabas' Church to-morrow evening. PRIVATE A. MONKS. Hero Of The Great Charge Now Reported Killed. Official news has recently reached Mr. James Monks, 91, Sharp-street, that his son, Private Arthur Monks, of the Warrington Territorials, who has been reported missing since the big engagement at Hoop on June 16th lase year, was killed in action, presumably on that date. Comrades over on leave said that Private

PRIVATE ARTHUR MONKS. Reported Killed.

Monks and several others were buried in the debris caused by a bursting shell. Private Monks, who would have celebrated his twentieth birthday next month', was for- merly employed by the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd., at their Grappenhall Works. He was educated at Heatheide School. and enlisted in September, 1914.

cated at Heathside School. He is a keen and able boxer, and recently secured a first prize in a contest at the ront, where he had been almost four months when he was ,wounded. His brother, Thomas, is on active service with the R.F.A. PRIVATE H. SUMNER. One Of A Patriotic Family Wounded In The Face. In an official casualty list published this week appears the name of Private Herbert Sumner, of the Royal Fusiliers (Public Schools Batta- lion), son of Mr. and Mrs. Robert Sumner, of 205, Orford-lane. He has been shot in the face, and is in hospital at Bristol, progressing fav- ourably. Private Sumner is one of three soldier- brothers, the other two being Sapper Arthur Stunner, of the Royal Engineers, and Sergeant Norman Sumner, of the R..A.M.C. In order to enlist, Private Herbert left the Mersey-street Gas Offices about thirteen months ago. He was a book-keeper. He has been on active service for about seven months. He was educated at Hamilton-street School. His father, who is 66 years of age, is a member of the Volunteer ,Training Corps and a Special Constable; while three: other members of the family attested, but could not be released from business. A seventh member offered his services soon after the Outbreak of war, but was rejected.

ADMIRAL JELLICOE Says What The Fleet Did. In reply to the congratulations of Admiral Fraser, of Whinnieknowe, Nairn, on the recent North Sea fight, Admiral Jellicoe says:- 1 expected a great deal of the Fleet, and it did all that I expected. If it had not been for our bad luck in the way of misty weather, we should have finished the business, I believe. •

The local Caledonian team met the Paelgate Bowling team on the Brooklands green last Tues- day evening, when a well-contested match proved interesting to the supporters of both teams. In the singles the Scotsmen suffered defeat by 43 points, but made a gallant stand in the doubles, and won by 20 points. The Padgate team took the laurels in the aggregate by 23 points. Sugary foods are bad for sufferers from rheu- matism, of which complaint "tender feet" is a variety. Sugar is bad for the rheumaticky, as it prevents the blood from clearing itself of waste matter. When we eat manufactured sugar, or when we eat foods into which artifi- cial sugar has been put, then we set up trouble. The sugar circulates in the blood, stops tissue changes, and has to be got rid of before the purifying action of the system can take place. Hence its badness. So sufferers from rheu- matism in the form of tender feet must ab- stain from sugar, jam, marmalade, chocolates, ices, sweets, cake, sweet pastry, and all foods and drinks to which artificial sugar has been added. Honey being very sugary, is also for- bidden.—" Reynolds' Newspaper."

"An Army Fights On Its Stomach" If men are to fight well they must feed well. And not only must they have access to good food but they must have digestive systems that Can easily assimilate it. An army of dyspeptics would be little more efficient than an army of starving men. The same principle holds good in other spheres of human endeavour. In business, for example, where competitiqn grows keener every year, no man can hope to snake a permanent success who labours under the handicap of indigestion. The successful man is just the average man plus some extra energy, well directed, and energy Is maintained by good food well digested.
